Compiled languages (go, rust, c, java) are not runners: they need a compile step and its cache.

## tool_smith starts its interpreter before the call (gate standby; Go only, 2026-11-03)

- **Why.** A tool_smith run took 16.3 ms (21.7 ms with one nested tool call), of which bun's own start is 4.5 ms; the rest was loading and compiling the prelude, which the wrapper imported only once the call's code was there. User decision: keep tool_smith TypeScript-only and separate from exec, and make it fast.
- **Entry `main.ts`.** The interpreter now runs `main.ts` (embedded `tool_smith_main.ts`): it imports `prelude.ts`, reads `/dev/fd/3` to its end, then imports `wrapper.ts`, which is unchanged (the call's code, at the same lines). A fresh start gets `/dev/null` as fd 3 (`extraFiles` on the child) and goes on at once; a waiting interpreter (standby) gets a pipe, which the call closes once it has written `wrapper.ts`, `input.json` and the staged functions. Both runs take the same path, so they give the same result. bun refuses to import a module from a named pipe, so exec's pipe standby could not be used. `/dev/fd/3` is read by path because deno does not read an inherited descriptor by number; bun, node and deno all run it.
- **Prelude.** The input and the function manifest are read when the run starts (`deliver`, first `fn` use), not when the module loads, since a standby loads the prelude before they exist. The exported `input` constant is gone; the body still receives `input` as its parameter.
- **Bridge token.** A standby gets its bridge socket path (inside its own directory) and a token in its environment when it starts; the call's bridge listens with that token (`startToolSmithBridgeWithToken`). The key leaves the token out and includes whether the run has a bridge, so an unbridged run never takes a bridged standby.
- **Pool.** The same process-wide pool as exec, keyed by argv, working directory, environment and the static files; a gate standby has fd 3 only and does not answer. tool_smith's directories keep their own rules (`makeToolSmithPrivateDir`: `tool-smith-` prefix, short fallback path for the socket, mode 0700). Settings `standby_pool` (1), `standby_idle_ms` (300000) and `standby_max` (8, counted together with exec's) in `tools.native.tool_smith`.
- **Same output as before.** Rendered results of the same scripts on the previous commit and on this one differ only in `prelude.ts` line numbers inside stack traces (the prelude changed); no `main.ts` frame appears. `TestToolSmithStandbyRunsLikeAFreshStart` compares fresh and standby runs over output, returned values, errors, exit codes, input and saved functions; the bridge test fails if the call's bridge makes a new token (checked).
- **Measured** (median of 20 runs through `executeToolSmith`, bun, 200 ms apart): without nested tools 16.3 ms before, 17.1 ms fresh now, 4.75 ms on standby; with one nested call 21.7, 22.9 and 10.9 ms. A fresh start is about 1 ms slower (the entry module and the fd 3 read).
- **Not proven.** bun 1.4 only for the timings (node and deno were checked for the same results, not timed through the tool). The 6 ms a nested call adds was not broken down (bridge start, connection, catalog call).

## A background sub-agent's answer reaches async whole; async takes block_ms as wait_ms (Go only, 2026-11-03)

- **The bug.** A local sub-agent run that ended after its call returned a run id gave `async wait` only the first `summary_max_bytes` (512) of its answer and `async status` the first `status_summary_max_bytes` (4096); the rest was only in the run's `messages.jsonl` (`ref`). A run that ended within the call returns up to `result_max_bytes` (32 KiB), and `profile_message` and remote sub-agent handles already kept their answer in the record (`Result.Detail`), which wait and status deliver up to the response budget (`response_max_bytes` minus the envelope and one entry's overhead, 6912 bytes by default). With `default_block_ms` at 10 s most real runs end in the background, so the usual path lost the answer.
- **The fix.** `subagentAsyncResult` sets `Detail` to the summary text it already built (changed files, then the answer, or the partial text, or the failure reason), as the other two kinds do. `asyncmgr.Manager.Status` asks the driver's `Detailer` only when the record holds no `Detail`: a stored result outranks the live view, which is cut to the status limit. Shell, exec, tool_smith and process handles store no `Detail`, so their status is unchanged; a profile_message or remote run whose run object outlived its end now shows its stored answer instead of `Status()`. Records written before this change have no `Detail` and keep the old behaviour.
- **Still cut.** An answer over the response budget (about 6.9 KB by default) is still cut in wait and status, against 32 KiB inline; `ref` names the transcript for the rest. Raising the async budget, or paging a result, is not part of this change.
- **block_ms.** Every start tool calls its wait `block_ms`; `async` calls it `wait_ms` (design_async_manager.md Q2), and a wait sent with `block_ms` got a bare `additionalProperties` refusal. The normalizer now takes `block_ms` as `wait_ms` when `wait_ms` is absent (as it takes `kill`/`cancel` as `stop`), so `{"block_ms":N}` alone is also an inferred wait. Sent beside `wait_ms`, `block_ms` is refused through `RejectedProperties` with a hint naming `wait_ms`, not one value chosen silently. The schema and description are unchanged. Four tests that sent `block_ms` to `async` (where Execute ignored it and used the default wait) now send `wait_ms`.
- **Tests.** `TestSubagentBackgroundAnswerIsDeliveredWholeByWaitAndStatus` (a 4.9 KB answer; without `Detail` wait gives 512 and status 4096 bytes, without the status precedence status gives 4096, both checked) and `TestAsyncToolTakesBlockMsAsWaitMs` (through the catalog; fails without the alias and without the hint, both checked).

## subagent and profile_message wait 120 s by default; async waits 120 s by default (Go only, 2026-11-03, user decision)

- **Why.** One `tools.native.async` `default_block_ms` (10 s) served every start tool. A shell command usually ends within it, a model run usually does not, so a sub-agent or profile message without `block_ms` nearly always came back as an id, and `async wait` without `wait_ms` returned after 10 s, so the model polled. A 60 s sub-agent took a start call, several waits and a read of the rest of the answer.
- **default_block_ms per tool.** `tools.native.subagent.default_block_ms` and `tools.native.profile_message.default_block_ms` (default 120000, 1 to 21600000) are the block of a call that sends no `block_ms`, clamped to `async` `max_block_ms` (120000 by default, so the default is the cap). `block_ms` in the call still wins, and 0 still returns an id at once. Settings built in code with zero use the async `default_block_ms`, as before (`agentBlock`). bash, exec, process and tool_smith keep the async default. A writer that runs to its end and a session without the async tool are unchanged.
- **default_wait_ms 120000** (was 10000). A wait returns as soon as a handle finishes, so a long default costs nothing when work ends early; it blocks the full time only when nothing ends, for example a wait over a server that never exits. When `default_wait_ms` is not set and `max_wait_ms` is lower, the default is `max_wait_ms`, so a configuration that only lowered `max_wait_ms` stays valid; one that sets `default_wait_ms` above `max_wait_ms` is still refused.
- **The trade-off.** While a call blocks, the model does nothing else in that round. A model that wants to work meanwhile passes `block_ms` 0 or a small `wait_ms`.
- **A call that holds its batch back waits the short default (found by the impact trace, fixed after the commit).** The first version said calls of one round block in parallel; that holds only when they run together. With `tool_call_scheduling` `ordered` (the default), a response with a call that may change files runs one call at a time, and `profile_message` always counts as such a call (`MayChangeFiles`), as does a writer sub-agent, `bash`, `edit`, `write` and every MCP tool. Two `profile_message` calls in one response then started up to 120 s apart instead of 10 s, and a read-only sub-agent beside a `bash` call held it back the same way; a scratch test of `executeToolRound` measured an ordered batch with a writer at the sum of its calls. Now `executeToolRound` sets `ExecutionContext.BatchWaiting` on every call but the last when the batch runs fewer calls at once than it has (concurrency 1, or fewer slots than calls), and `agentBlock` then uses the async `default_block_ms` instead of the tool's own; an explicit `block_ms` still wins, and a call that is alone, last, or runs together with the rest keeps 120 s. `ForNested` clears the flag: a nested call (a tool_smith script's, or a child's) is not in the caller's batch, and the caller's own block already bounds how long the batch waits. Tests: `TestToolRoundTellsACallWhenLaterCallsWaitBehindIt` (session) and the held-batch cases of `TestAgentBlockUsesTheToolsOwnDefault` (native); each fails with its half of the fix removed (checked).
- **Tests.** `TestSubagentAndProfileMessageDefaultBlock`, `TestNativeAsyncDefaultWaitFollowsALowerMaxWait` (sessionfs); `TestAgentBlockUsesTheToolsOwnDefault`, `TestSubagentCallWithoutBlockMsWaitsItsDefaultBlock` (native; both fail when the calls ignore the setting, checked). A test in `app` sent `block_ms` to `async` Execute, which ignored it and used the default wait (10 s, now up to 120 s); it sends `wait_ms` 0 now, as it meant to (0.36 s instead of 10 s).
- **Not proven.** How many rounds a real model saves; that depends on how long its sub-agents run and on whether it sets `block_ms` itself.

## load_tool names the loaded tools instead of repeating their definitions (Go only, 2026-11-03)

- **Why.** A loaded lazy tool is listed (`WithLazyGate`'s `Listed`) in the tools array of every later request, rebuilt each round (`session/runner.go`), so the request that carries the `load_tool` result already carries the full definition. The result printed the description and `inputSchema` as well, so each definition sat in the history as a second copy for the rest of the session.
- **Now.** A load answers `Loaded. Available, with the full definition, from the next request.` and one `name — summary` line per tool (the same summary as the listing, `tools.lazy_summary_bytes`), marked `(already loaded)` for a tool loaded before this call; the sentence is still left out when nothing new was loaded. Sections per pattern, `already listed above`, the no-match lines and `batch_too_large` are unchanged. The description in Go and in `sample/tools/native/load_tool.json` says the loaded tool comes with its full definition in the next request; the stale comment that loading does not change the tool list is corrected.
- **Size.** Measured on native definitions with a 200-byte summary: transcript 3507 → 215 bytes, profile 1910 → 212, subagent 1541 → 213, async 1143 → 210.
- **messages.jsonl.** The structure is unchanged; the text of a `load_tool` result written from now on is shorter. Results already in a transcript are untouched.
- **Tests.** `TestLoadToolResultSaysAvailableFromTheNextRequest` (exact text, and the already-loaded mark), `TestLazyOverlappingPatternsShowAToolOnce` (the line once, no schema in the history).

## transcript keeps a turn index between calls and lists from the end (Go only, 2026-11-03)

- **Why.** Every call decoded every line of the file to find where turns start, and a list (the default call, its cursor pages, the role and tool filters) then walked the whole selected range to keep its newest steps. On a 98 MB transcript (2400 turns): 1.36 s per default list or page, 0.31–0.33 s for `turns:-1` or one `step`, 1.41 s for a search.
- **Prefilter.** The indexer decodes a line only when it contains `"session_turn"`, or both `"role"` and `"user"` (`transcriptLineMayHead`); other lines decode to an empty head anyway. Transcripts are written by encoding/json, which writes those keys and the value as plain bytes; a key written in another case (which `json.Unmarshal` would also match) or as a `\u` escape is not recognised. `TestTranscriptIndexMatchesADecodeOfEveryLine` compares the table with a decode of every line.
- **Index cache.** `tools.native.transcript.index_cache_entries` (default 32, 0 to 4096; 0 decodes the whole file each call) is how many turn tables the process keeps, keyed by path and `line_max_bytes`, least recently used dropped first. A table is reused when the file's identity (hash of its first line, as cursors use) is the same, it is not shorter than the table's end, and the 4 KiB before that end hash the same, so a front-compaction that cut the file and let it grow past the old end is caught; the call then parses from the start of the table's last turn, which an append may extend or renumber (`indexTranscriptTurnsFrom`, as `async_latest` does). Tables are replaced, never changed in place.
- **Backward list.** A list without `search` reads whole turns from the end of its range (or from its cursor's turn) back over a 1 MiB window that doubles until the page is decided: the read reached the first turn, or it holds `max_list_entries`+1 selected steps and the steps it read in the page's turns hold no result without its call. The walk pairs a result with its call across turns, so a late result read without its call would add a step and shift numbers; a call on the page without a result, when the read stopped at the cursor's turn, widens it to the end of the range. Cursors, headers and step numbers are unchanged. A search still reads the whole range. A sparse filter can read up to about twice the range before deciding.
- **Same output.** `TestTranscriptBackwardListMatchesAWholeRead` follows every cursor of nine listings (none, turn ranges, role and tool filters) over generated transcripts with late results, unanswered calls, orphan results, multi-message batches, over-long and unreadable lines and compaction numbering, at windows of 1 and 3000 bytes and two page limits, and compares 2034 pages byte for byte with a read of the whole range. It fails when either widening rule is removed; the cache test fails without the tail check or with a wrong resume number (all checked). `TestTranscriptIndexCacheUnderConcurrentReadsAndAppends` reads with the cache on while a writer appends (clean under `-race`).
- **Measured** (98 MB generated transcript, warm page cache, median of 5, previous commit → now): default list 1.36 s → 14 ms; next page 1.36 s → 15 ms; `turns:-1` 306 → 1 ms; `step` 316–329 → 1 ms; `tool:edit` 1.37 s → 37 ms; search 1.41 → 1.08 s. The first call on a file builds the index: 118 ms (was part of every call).
- **Not proven.** A cold page cache; real transcripts, whose lines are mostly larger `response.completed` records the prefilter cannot skip; memory of the cache beyond 16 bytes per turn per table.

## Core tool definitions say each thing once (Go only, 2026-11-03)

- **What.** Text a definition said twice, in its description and in a property, is kept in one place: `transcript` (the description listed the path forms and every filter again; the four remote path forms are one rule: `remote:<name>/` before a `profile:` or `subagent:` path that names its session), `transcripts` (the remote forms and "listed by the profile tool" twice), `profile` (the compact/reset rules in the description and in `action`), `subagent` (the resume details), `profile_message` (`block_ms` repeated the busy-target explanation). No fact was dropped. Sample manifests and the Go copies changed together.
- **Size** (description plus compact schema, before → after): transcript 3417 → 2886 bytes, transcripts 1745 → 1443, profile 1845 → 1575, profile_message 1040 → 970, subagent 1483 → 1404; the seven core tools 11281 → 10067 bytes (load_tool's text grew by 38 bytes in the commit before). These definitions go with every request.
- **Restart with the files.** The `inputSchema` of transcript, transcripts, profile_message and load_tool changed, and a definition file must match the binary's schema exactly in both directions (see the `stdin` entry for bash): a backend that is still the old binary refuses the new files, so restart it right after these files are in place.
- **Three tests never checked what they claimed.** `requireParentReceivedProfileMessageOutput` (app) searched whole request bodies, which also carry the tool definitions: "answered" matched the old `profile_message.block_ms` text and "handle" the `async` description. The remote case actually failed (`JAD_REMOTE_E2E` was not in the fixture's environment) and the handled case expected a word its output never has. The helper now searches only `function_call_output` items, the layout fixture's environment has `JAD_REMOTE_E2E`, and the handled case expects `use the async tool with id pm_`; all three pass on the real outputs.

## The Web UI projection reads each byte fewer times (Go only, 2026-11-03)

Measured with in-tree benchmarks (`internal/webui/normalize_benchmark_test.go`, `internal/httpapi/event_pipeline_benchmark_test.go`), 4 CPUs, median of 3, before → after. The frames, messages and transcript are unchanged: equivalence tests compare every result with the code it replaced.

- **Live stream: the message summaries follow the deltas.** `StreamingRun.Message` ran `FieldsFromParts` on every delta, which joins every text and reasoning part of the run again, so a run's cost grew with the square of its text once it had more than one step. The run now keeps the join up to date (`summaryCache`): a delta appended to the last part extends it, any other change to a text or reasoning part rebuilds it at the next snapshot, and a kind with one part uses that part directly (no copy). One delta into a 20-step, 100 KB message: 38.6 → 6.4 µs and 132 → 15 KB allocated (`StreamingRunMultiStepDeltas`); 50 steps, 200 KB: 80 → 15.9 µs, 261 → 42 KB; the executor's whole per-delta path with 20 text parts: 48.8 → 9.9 µs, 120 → 11 KB (`StreamEventPipelineLongText`). One-part messages are unchanged (1.7 µs). What is left grows with the part count: the snapshot copies the parts, which `TestStreamingRunMessageDoesNotAliasRunParts` requires. Tests: `TestStreamingRunSummariesMatchTheParts`, `TestStreamingRunSummariesMatchThePartsInRandomRuns` (each fails when any one of the three cache updates is removed).
- **History: one decode per tool output.** A `function_call_output` was scanned up to five times: the UI text tried a list, then a string, and the stored-image and inline-image counts each tried a list again; a plain-string output failed the three list attempts only after scanning all of it. `readToolOutput` looks at the first byte: a string is unquoted once, a list is decoded once into typed parts that feed all three. Anything else, and a list the typed decode refuses (an entry that is not an object, a field that is not a string), takes the former per-field helpers, so those give the result they gave before. `ToolResultPart` (live tool results) reuses the decoded list for its status. 50 turns with 200 KB text outputs: 236 → 86 ms, 67 → 34 MB; with 200 KB inline images: 150 → 84 ms, 44 → 22 MB; 1 KB outputs: 2.83 → 2.07 ms. The remainder is `encoding/json` validating, scanning and unquoting each output once at record level and once for the output. **Limit:** the typed decode matches keys case-insensitively, so an output entry spelled `"Text"` instead of `"text"` now shows its text; the old UI text read only the exact key (the image counts already matched either way). Test: `TestReadToolOutputMatchesTheFormerProjection` compares the old code, kept in the test, on 23 outputs including malformed ones.
- **Inline image fetch: only the asked tool result reads its output.** `FindInlineImage` normalized the whole turn, decoding every image of it, to find one record. The others now join their message as an empty part; grouping never reads an output, so message ids and steps are the same. Last image of a turn with ten 2 MB images: 325 → 108 ms, 90 → 29 MB; one image: 49 → 36 ms. Test: `TestProjectingOneCallGroupsMessagesAsAFullProjection`.
- **History route and snapshot: the backup is read only when it changed.** The history ETag depends on file stamps only (backup stamp, transcript identity, `backup_turns`), but `/history` read and normalized the whole backup before comparing `If-None-Match`, and every first-page snapshot did the same to report the block's message count. `/history` now answers 304 from the stamps, and the snapshot keeps the count with its ETag on the shared session (one entry per session, released with the session's store), so it reads the backup again only after a compaction or a new transcript file. The ETag is now taken before the backup is read: a backup replaced in between yields a newer block under the older ETag, which the next request refetches; before, an older block could be stored under the newer ETag and revalidate as current. Snapshot with a 50-turn backup of 20 KB outputs: 15.7 ms, 10 MB → 2.8 µs, 1 KB when the count is kept (both measured with the new projection; the old path was slower still). Tests: `TestHistoryRouteAnswersAMatchingETagWithoutReadingTheBackup` (an unreadable backup still gets 304), `TestHistoryBlockETagIsTheStampVersion`, `TestSnapshotHistoryReferenceKeepsTheCountWithItsETag`.
- **Impact trace (after the commit).** Consumers outside `webui`: the run executor (`StreamingRun`, `ToolResultPart` through `AddToolResult`), `keepLiveImages` (then `ToolResultPart` for the image count and `InlineImage` per image; since af4617e the executor's one projection and one `InlineImages` read, see the next entry), the snapshot and page routes (`NormalizeTranscript`), `ReadMessageImage` (`FindInlineImage`). The history count lives on the shared session, which is dropped when its store goes idle; two profiles of one transcript with different `backup_turns` have different ETags and simply replace each other's entry. One regression found and fixed: a rebuilt summary came from a `strings.Builder` grown by doubling, and the run's last committed message keeps that text for the whole retention window, so a multi-part message could hold up to twice the joined text where `strings.Join` allocated it exactly. The rebuild now sizes the builder first (`TestStreamingRunSummaryRebuildAllocatesTheJoinOnce`: 18 allocations without the sizing, 2 with it). `-race` passes for `webui`, `app` and `httpapi`.
- **Not done.** The per-delta event decode (`StreamingRun.Update` decodes each event into a map, and the executor decodes text deltas a second time for the content cache; 1.7 µs and 20 allocations per delta) and the double `Increments` of the emitter (2.5 % of the 20-part profile once shared strings short-circuit the comparisons): both are small next to the provider's token rate. `readBackupTail` still decodes every backup record into a map to find turn starts, but with the count kept it runs only when the backup changed.
- **Not proven.** Real transcripts and real browsers; GC pauses under concurrent runs; a cold page cache for the backup read.

## Live tool results are projected once and their images decoded once; the history ETag names the inline cap (Go only, 2026-11-03)

Three items the impact trace of the Web UI projection commit listed as already there before it.

- **One projection per live tool result.** The executor projected each result twice, once for `keepLiveImages` and once for the message, and the first used position 0 for a result with no `call_id`, so its images were kept under `tool-call-0` while the message part (and so the browser) named `tool-call-<position>`: the live image route found nothing. `StreamingRun.ProjectToolResult` now returns the part `AddToolResult` would add, `publishToolResult` keeps the images under that part's call id and then adds the same part. The order is unchanged: images are kept before the message that counts them is published. A result without a `call_id` is not produced by today's runner, which always copies the call's id, so the wrong key was reachable only through that edge. Test: `TestToolResultImagesAreKeptUnderTheCallIDTheMessageShows` (fails with the old key).
- **One read of the record for all its images.** `keepLiveImages` called `InlineImage` per image, and each call decoded the whole record, so a result with N images was read N times. `webui.InlineImages` decodes the record once and returns every image, failing as a whole when any one would fail (as the loop did). One result with ten 2 MB inline images: 2.8 s, 693 MB → 0.49 s, 133 MB (the old sequence measured with the new single-pass projection). Test: `TestInlineImagesDecodesEveryImageAsInlineImageDoes`.
- **The history ETag includes `server.max_inline_part_bytes`.** The block is served cut at that cap, but the ETag did not name it, so after the cap changed and the backend restarted, a browser that held a block cut at the old cap was told it was current until the next compaction. The snapshot's history reference carries the same ETag, so the frontend still matches the two. Every browser refetches its history block once after this change. Test: `TestHistoryETagChangesWithTheInlineCap` (fails without the cap in the key).
- **Impact trace (after the commit).** `keepLiveImages` has one caller, `publishToolResult`, which runs from the runner's serialized publish, after the provider stream of the round ended, so nothing changes the run between `ProjectToolResult` and `AddToolResultPart`. The part count and `InlineImages` read the same list for every record the runner writes; a mismatch (only a record with both `output` and a differently cased `Output` key) now keeps no image where the old loop kept the first ones. Peak memory per result is unchanged: the old loop also held every data URL of the record on each pass, and the decoded images were kept either way. The frontend asks the live route with the part's call id, which is now the stored key; after the run, the stored message's own part ids go to the transcript route as before. The frontend holds history blocks in memory only and compares the snapshot's history ETag with the one it stored from the same snapshot, so the new ETag means one refetch per open session after the upgrade. No other Go code computes the history ETag.

## The transcript reader checks JSON while it splits it (Go only, 2026-11-03)

Transcript reads (`expandTranscriptRecord` for run start and history, compaction, turn commits) passed each record through `objectFields`, which ran `json.Valid` over the whole line and then split it again with a byte-by-byte scanner; nested values were then scanned once more per nesting level while canonicalized. `json.Valid` was 19 % of the history-read profile and the scanner 27 %. Third-party JSON libraries were considered first (sonic reads 3–5× faster than `encoding/json`), but they change output defaults, depend on Go internals and add unsafe code to the parsing of untrusted tool output; reading each byte fewer times needs none of that.

- **One pass for the record.** `objectFields` validates each key and value while splitting the object (`splitObjectFields` with `validate`), using `validJSON`'s rules (`jsonvalid.go`): the same grammar, escapes, control-byte rule, invalid-UTF-8 acceptance and 10000-level nesting limit as `encoding/json`. String bodies are checked eight bytes at a time. `arrayValues` and `canonicalJSON` call `validJSON` instead of `json.Valid`. The error text is unchanged ("expected a JSON object").
- **Strings already checked are skipped quote to quote.** `scanJSONString` jumps with `bytes.IndexByte` and counts the backslashes before each quote; the container scan jumps over strings with it. This is the rescan per nesting level, now at memory speed for long text.
- **The cursor scan drops its `json.Valid`.** `scanCursorRuns` ran `json.Valid` and then `json.Unmarshal`, which refuses invalid JSON itself.
- **Measured** (`go test -bench`, 4 CPUs, 6 runs, benchstat, all p=0.002): run start 100/1000/5000 turns −54 to −57 % (1000 turns shared 23.8 → 10.8 ms), uncached history read 5000 turns 113.8 → 49.5 ms, `RunRoundsHistory` −38 to −56 %, `ReadInputItems1000Records` 2.58 → 1.82 ms; per record 1.5 KB user 10.6 → 4.4 µs, completed 16.4 → 5.4 µs, 20 KB tool output 78 → 27 µs, 2 KB text with many escapes 28.9 → 24.8 µs (its escapes still go through `json.Unmarshal` to canonicalize). Allocations and bytes unchanged.
- **Tests.** `TestValidJSONMatchesEncodingJSON` compares `validJSON` with `json.Valid`, and the one-pass `objectFields` with `json.Valid` plus the former split, on seeds and 200000 random mutations; `TestValidJSONKeepsTheNestingLimit`; `TestValidStringEndChecksEveryWordPosition` (each special byte at every offset of the eight-byte check); `TestScanJSONStringMatchesTheFormerScanAfterBackslashRuns`; `FuzzValidJSONMatchesEncodingJSON` (4 million inputs in a 90 s run, no difference). Removing the trailing-comma check, the nesting limit, the control-byte lane, the backslash parity or the end-of-input check each fails one of them. The canonical output, and so `messages.jsonl`, is unchanged: the existing fixture tests pass, and the code that writes canonical JSON (`canonicalJSONValid`, `encodeJSONObject`, `appendJSONString`) was not changed.
- **Impact trace (after the commit).** The changed functions are unexported, so every caller is in `internal/transcript`; `httpapi/run_body_scan.go` has its own scanner and is untouched. Callers of `objectFields` (record reads, `readTurnManifest`, turn commits, compaction, `ValidateInputItem`, which `session/runner.go` calls), `arrayValues` and `canonicalJSON` get the same accept/refuse result and error text as before. The scanners that changed (`scanJSONString`, the container scan) are reached only through the `*Valid` variants, and each of those is given bytes already checked: a sub-value of a record `objectFields` split, or an item of a compaction cache `json.Unmarshal` read. Finding a string's end counts each backslash run once, before the one quote that ends it, so the jump stays linear on strings made of escapes. The eight-byte check only asks whether any byte of a word is special, so it gives the same answer on big-endian CPUs. Extra cases, old → new (6 runs, benchstat): 300 small fields 126 → 113 µs, a 58-byte record 1.13 → 0.94 µs, 140 KB of escaped quotes 1.72 → 1.46 ms, 2000 nested levels 78.6 ms both (unchanged). That last cost was already there and comes from canonicalization, which scans each level's contents again, so it grows with the square of the depth; this commit did not change it.
- **Not proven.** Real transcripts (the benchmarks use generated turns), other CPUs (the eight-byte check is portable Go; `bytes.IndexByte` is vectorized on amd64 and arm64), and cold page cache, where reading the file may dominate.

## `jad run`: one turn without a server (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** The only way to drive a run was the HTTP API of a running server, so CI jobs, scripts and evals needed a server, a token and an SSE client.
- **Now.** `jad run --session <ref> [--profile] [--model] [--format text|json|jsonl] [--timeout] [prompt | -]` builds the same service as the server (`app.NewHeadless`) and sends `POST /api/sessions/{ref}/run` to its handler in-process (`internal/headless`): the body streams through an `io.Pipe`, nothing listens. A headless run therefore takes the same session and transcript locks (so it waits for, or queues behind, a server's run of the same profile), writes the same `messages.jsonl` and emits the same events as a web run. `text` prints the final answer, `json` one result object (`status`, `text`, `turn_id`, `usage`, `error`, `duration_ms`), `jsonl` every run-stream event as `{"event","data"}` and then `{"event":"result"}`. Exit status: 0 completed, 1 failed or refused, 2 usage, 124 timed out, 130 interrupted.
- **What differs from the server.** The API token is random and never leaves the process (no `JAD_API_TOKEN` needed); the remote API is off; the start-time recovery scans (`recover_processes_at_start`, `recover_subagent_runs_at_start`) are off, because they inspect every session's processes and sub-agent runs, which is the long-running server's job. Logs go to `server.log_file` or stderr, never stdout; `--log-level` overrides `server.log_level`.
- **Stopping.** `--timeout` and SIGINT/SIGTERM call the stop route, as the web client's stop button does, and wait for the run's terminal events; child processes the run started end with the service, as at a server shutdown. A run has no time limit by default: `max_tool_rounds` and `max_run_tokens` still apply.
- **Not covered.** `request_draft` questions: a headless run does not set `supports_interaction`, so the model is not offered the question tool. Images and files in the prompt (the run body carries text only).
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged: the run goes through the same route and runner.
- **Tests.** `internal/headless` (SSE parsing with keep-alives, failed, cut and empty streams, refusals with the server's message, one stop call on timeout and on cancel; `-race`), `internal/app/headless_test.go` (completed run writes the transcript and reports usage; requested model; 404 and 400 refusals; timeout cancels the provider request; cancel aborts and frees the session), `cmd/jad/headless_test.go` (the three formats, stdin prompt, usage-error and refusal exit codes). Checked by hand with the built binary against a fake provider: exit 0, 124 (`--timeout 1s` on a silent provider) and 130 (SIGINT).

## `jad eval`: suites of tasks scored by checks (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** Nothing measured whether a prompt, tool or model change makes the agent better or worse; the `messages.jsonl` goldens only catch format regressions.
- **Now.** `jad eval [--out] [--cases] [--repeat] [--parallel] <suite.json>` (`internal/evalrun`, format `sample/schemes/eval-suite.schema.json`, example `sample/evals/smoke`). Each attempt gets a fresh folder with a copy of the case's `workspace`, runs its `setup` commands, writes a session file `eval-<suite>-<run id>-<case>-<attempt>.json` (category `eval`, `cwd` the workspace, other keys from the suite's `session`) into a session root, runs one headless turn, then runs its `checks` in the workspace and matches its `expect_text` patterns against the answer. Pass: the run completed, every check exited 0, every pattern matched. Fail: the agent ran but one of those did not hold (checks still run after a timed-out run). Error: setup, the session file or the run request failed. The report (stdout or `--out`) lists every attempt with its checks' exit codes and output tail, the session reference and the token usage, and sums tokens and the pass rate; progress lines go to stderr. Exit 0 only when every attempt passed.
- **Kept for inspection.** The case sessions and their transcripts stay (they show in the web UI under category `eval`); workspaces follow `keep_workspaces` (default `failed`).
- **The run id** is the start time plus 6 random hex digits: two evals started in the same second wrote the same session names and the second one's files were refused (found by `TestEvalCommandWritesTheReportAndFailsOnAFailedCase`; `TestRunIDsDifferWithinOneSecond`).
- **Checks** run with `shell` (default `sh -c`) in their own process group, killed as a group at `check_timeout_ms`, with `JAD_EVAL_SUITE`, `JAD_EVAL_CASE`, `JAD_EVAL_ATTEMPT`, `JAD_EVAL_WORKSPACE` and `JAD_EVAL_ANSWER_FILE` set.
- **Defaults** live in `evalrun` constants and in the schema's `default`s; `TestSuiteSchemaDefaultsMatchTheLoader` and `TestSampleSuiteAndSchemaAgreeWithTheLoader` keep the two and the sample in step.
- **Tests.** `internal/evalrun` (workspace copy with modes, setup before the agent, session file content, check and pattern outcomes, retention, repeat, filter, the parallel bound, check timeout killing background children, output tail, interrupt; `-race`), `cmd/jad` (report file, exit codes, a filtered run passing). One bug found by them: the workspace-kept flag and duration were set in deferred code on a non-named result and never reached the report.
- **Not proven.** Scores with a real model: the sample suite was run only against a fake provider (both cases fail there, as they should).

## `server.tracing`: OpenTelemetry spans over OTLP/HTTP JSON (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **What.** With `server.tracing.enabled`, the server, `jad run` and `jad eval` record one span per run (`jad.run`, in `httpapi.executeRun`), per model request (`chat <model>`, around `runResponse`) and per tool call (`execute_tool <name>`, in `executeToolRound`), all children of the run span. Attributes follow the OpenTelemetry GenAI conventions (`gen_ai.operation.name`, `gen_ai.request.model`, `gen_ai.response.id`, `gen_ai.usage.input_tokens`, `...cache_read.input_tokens`, `...reasoning.output_tokens`, `gen_ai.tool.name`, `gen_ai.tool.call.id`) plus `jad.session`, `jad.profile`, `jad.turn_id`, `jad.round`, `jad.run.status`, `jad.tool.output_bytes`. Spans never carry prompts, answers, tool arguments, tool output or error text: a failed span has `error.type` (`canceled`, `deadline_exceeded` or the Go error type) only. Keys: `endpoint`, `service_name` (`jad`), `sample_ratio` (1, parent-based), `export_timeout_ms` (10000), `batch_timeout_ms` (5000), `max_queue_size` (2048); headers come from `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` / `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_HEADERS`, and without `endpoint` the `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_(TRACES_)ENDPOINT` variables apply. The process flushes at exit with `export_timeout_ms` as the deadline, after the service has closed, so the last runs' spans are sent.
- **Our own exporter.** The OpenTelemetry API and SDK are used; the OTLP exporter is ours (`tracing/otlpjson.go`, OTLP/HTTP with the JSON encoding). The project's protobuf exporter (`otlptracehttp`) links grpc and protobuf, whose reflection keeps every exported method of the binary alive: `jad` went from 34.9 MB to 68.3 MB with it, and is 36.3 MB with the JSON exporter (+1.35 MB for the SDK and the instrumentation). OTLP/JSON is a stable part of OTLP 1.x and is accepted by the OpenTelemetry Collector, Jaeger and Tempo; it does not retry a refused batch (the batch is dropped and the error logged at warn).
- **Off costs nothing measurable.** Until a provider is installed the Start functions return a non-recording span without building attributes and the End functions skip every decode: `TestTracingOffAllocatesNothing` (0 allocations for a run, a round and a tool call).
- **Compatibility checked.** A body captured from a real `jad run` was decoded with the Collector's own `ptrace.JSONUnmarshaler` (go.opentelemetry.io/collector/pdata v1.31.0, in a throwaway module): ids, parent link, kind, status, integer and array attributes read back as sent. Running the Collector binary itself was not done.
- **Tests.** `internal/tracing` (nesting and attributes; no content and no error text in spans; zero allocations off; setup and restore; the OTLP/JSON body: grouping, hex ids, decimal-string integers, arrays, kinds, status; endpoint and header precedence; a refused export's error), `internal/sessionfs/tracing_settings_test.go` (defaults, refusals, schema defaults and bounds), `internal/app/tracing_integration_test.go` (a run with a tool call is one trace: run, two chat spans with usage, the tool span, and no file content).
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged: spans are recorded beside the run; no request or transcript byte depends on them.
- **Not proven.** Overhead with tracing on under load; behaviour of hosted collectors (auth headers, gzip is not sent).

## `api` in `model.json`: Anthropic and Chat Completions translated in process (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** A Claude model needed the separate `anthropic-adapter` process (a port, a second credential hop, one more thing to start), and a provider with only Chat Completions (vLLM, Ollama, llama.cpp, LM Studio, DeepSeek, Groq, most gateways) could not be used at all.
- **Now.** `model.json` `api` is `responses` (default, unchanged), `anthropic_messages` or `chat_completions`. For the last two, `newProviderStreamer` builds the same `openaiapi.Client` with an `inprocess.Transport` as its HTTP client: the transport answers each request with the translator's `http.Handler` on a goroutine and streams its body through an `io.Pipe`; closing the body cancels the handler. The translator calls the provider with the client `providerHTTPClient` built (network family, HTTP debug), so the debug log shows the real upstream exchange. Settings are checked when the model file loads (`sessionfs/model_api.go`), so `jad check` reports them.
- **Why a handler behind the Responses client, not a second `responses.Streamer`.** The client already holds `provider_retry` (opening-request retries with `Retry-After`), the idle guard and the HTTP-status error that the run reports. Both translators answer an upstream HTTP error with its status and `Retry-After` before any stream byte, so all of that applies unchanged; and the Anthropic adapter is reused byte for byte. The cost is one JSON encode and SSE parse per event in process, small next to a model call.
- **Anthropic.** `anthropicadapter.ParseEmbeddedConfig` reads the model file's `anthropic` object with the adapter's own decoder and validation; `listen` and `caller_token_env` are refused (nothing listens) and so is `upstream.base_url` (one place for the URL: the model file's `base_url`). With `upstream.credential_file` the run needs no `api_key_env` value. `api_key_env` defaults to `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`. Linking the adapter and the Anthropic SDK grows `jad` from 36.3 MB to 45.1 MB (measured, linux/amd64); the SDK is the transport only (see the 2026-10-11 decision), so replacing it with `net/http` would recover most of that, which was not done here (it was done later the same day: see "The Anthropic adapter sends with `net/http`").
- **Chat Completions (`internal/chatadapter`).** Instructions and system/developer messages become `system_role` messages; function calls join the assistant message before them; each call is answered by a tool message right after the assistant message, a call the history never answers gets `missing_tool_result_text`, and what cannot sit between tool messages (images a tool returned, a result whose call is not in the history) waits in one user message until the round is answered, because Chat Completions refuses anything else. Reasoning deltas (`reasoning_fields`, default `reasoning_content` then `reasoning`) become a reasoning item with `reasoning_text` content; `echo_reasoning_field` sends that text back on the next assistant message (DeepSeek's thinking mode with tools wants it); otherwise reasoning is dropped from the history. `max_output_tokens` goes to `max_tokens_field`, `reasoning.effort` to `reasoning_effort`, `text.format` to `response_format`; `store`, `include`, `metadata`, `prompt_cache_key` and other Responses-only fields are dropped. Hosted tools, `previous_response_id`, non-function `tool_choice` objects and unknown item types are refused with 400. A stream that ends after a finish reason without `[DONE]` completes; one that ends before fails without closing the half-built item; an in-stream `error` object fails the response with its code; `length` and `content_filter` give `incomplete`. A server that ignores `stream: true` and answers JSON is translated as one chunk.
- **Sample.** `sample/jad/models/cheap/haiku.json` had `provider: anthropic` and no `base_url`, so it sent an Anthropic key to the OpenAI endpoint; it is now `api: anthropic_messages` with `max_output_tokens` (its `max_tokens` was not a Responses field). `sample/model-chat-completions.json` is an Ollama example, kept outside `sample/jad` because the layout tests count that catalog's models.
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged for `api: responses`: the request, client and runner path are the same, and the fingerprint only gains fields. A translated model writes the Responses items its translator emits, the same shapes the external adapter produced.
- **Tests.** `internal/inprocess` (status, header and body; streaming before the handler returns; close cancels the handler; cancel before the status; a panic before and after the status; `-race`), `internal/chatadapter` (config defaults and refusals, schema in step with the loader, request fields, a history with parallel calls, an unanswered call, a tool image and an orphan result, refusals, stream translation with reasoning, text and two calls, endings, HTTP errors keeping status and `Retry-After`, a JSON answer, extra headers, an oversized event), `internal/anthropicadapter/embedded_test.go`, `internal/sessionfs/model_api_test.go` (api values, refusals, the samples load), `internal/app/provider_translator_test.go` (a headless run with a tool round through each translator against a fake upstream; a provider 400 fails the run with its status; credential_file needs no key). The schema change was checked with ajv 8 (draft 2020-12, all schemes registered as the catalog editor does): both samples and an OpenAI model valid, `listen`, `upstream.base_url`, an unknown `chat_completions` key and an unknown `api` invalid.
- **Not proven.** Nothing ran against a real Anthropic or Chat Completions server; the fakes follow the providers' documentation. Provider quirks beyond the documented fields (tool-call deltas without an index, reasoning in other fields, servers that refuse `stream_options` or `parallel_tool_calls`) are covered only where a key exists for them.

## Sub-agent `isolation: "worktree"`: a writer works in a git worktree and returns a branch (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** A sub-agent that changes files works in the session's own folder, so two of them at once can overwrite each other; that is why a writer runs to its end inside the call and `max_background_writers` defaults to 1. With a worktree each writer has a checkout of its own, so writers can run side by side and the parent decides what to keep.
- **Now.** An agent with `isolation: "worktree"` (default `"none"`; refused for an agent without a change tool) runs each run in `git worktree add -b <branch_prefix><run id> <dir>/<run id> HEAD` of the repository that holds the session's working directory (a session in a subfolder works in the same subfolder of the worktree). When the run ends — done, failed, timed out or stopped — after the child and its processes have stopped, whatever it left is committed with `git add --all` (the configured author only when the repository has no identity; `--no-verify` unless `commit_hooks`), the worktree folder is removed, and the branch is kept when it has commits beyond the base and deleted otherwise. The result names `worktree_branch`, `worktree_base`, `worktree_changes` (git's shortstat) and the commands to review, merge or drop it; the async summary carries the same line. A commit or cleanup that fails keeps the folder and reports `worktree_error` and `worktree_path`, so no work is lost. A resume starts its worktree at the branch the source run left. Settings: `tools.native.subagent.worktree` (`dir`, `branch_prefix` `jad/`, `git` `git`, `author_name` `jad`, `author_email` `jad@localhost`, `commit_hooks` false, `timeout_ms` 60000 per git command).
- **Where the worktrees live.** By default in `jad-worktrees` inside the repository's common git directory: never under the session's folder, where the parent's grep and glob would see a second copy of the project, and never in `/tmp`, which may be cleaned while a run goes.
- **Scheduled like a reader.** A run in a worktree changes no file of the session, so `ChangesWorkspace` (change tool and no isolation), not `ChangesFiles`, decides `MayChangeFiles`, the checkpoint, the background-writer place and the async handle. The changed-file list and the "can change files" result notes still use `ChangesFiles`.
- **Path rules follow the run.** `ExecutionContext.WorkspaceOrigin` holds the session's folder while `WorkspaceRoot` is the worktree. `workspacePolicy` moves every rule (and agent `paths` entry, via `movedScope` when the worktree opens) inside the origin to the same place in the worktree, and applies at the entry of read, glob, grep, bash, exec, process, tool_smith, read_image, write and edit; a rule inside the origin no longer reaches the origin, so an allow rule cannot let the run change the session's checkout through an absolute path. Rules outside the origin are kept.
- **Limits.** The worktree starts from a commit: the session's uncommitted changes are not in it. MCP servers run in the session's folder, not the worktree. A nested run that an isolated child started as a handle and left going is stopped when the child ends, but not waited for: what it writes after the commit is not on the branch. `paths` and `tools.deny` stay in-process limits; bash in the worktree can still reach any path (rule 0).
- **Fingerprint.** `NativeSubagentAgent.Isolation` is `omitempty` in JSON, so an agent without it keeps the fingerprint it had and a resume after the upgrade gets no config note.
- **Tests.** `internal/worktree` (branch kept with a change and dropped without; the session's uncommitted change stays out; the configured author only without an identity; the run's own commits kept; a subfolder; start from a branch; a missing revision, a plain folder, an empty repository and a duplicate name refused; 8 runs of one repository at once; a configured `dir`; `-race`), `internal/sessionfs/native_subagent_worktree_test.go` (defaults, values, refusals; the schema test covers the new keys), `internal/tools/native/subagent_worktree_test.go` (the real write tool in the worktree: a deny rule of the session holds there, the session's files and folder stay unchanged, the branch holds the change; a run without a change leaves no branch; outside a repository the run fails before the child starts; a resume sees the first run's change; the call is not `MayChangeFiles` and the list marks the agent; the run is an async handle whose wait names the branch; policy and scope moves), `internal/app/subagent_worktree_integration_test.go` (a headless run with the real tools: the branch holds the child's file, the session's folder does not, the parent reads the branch, no checkpoint, no worktree left). The schema change was checked with ajv 8.
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged for an agent without isolation: no request byte or result field differs. An isolated run's tool result carries the `worktree_*` information lines.
- **Not proven.** Behaviour with very large repositories (worktree add copies the checkout), with submodules or Git LFS, and with git older than 2.43 (the version tested).

## `model_fallback`: another model answers when the session's model is down (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** A run failed whenever its one model was rate-limited or overloaded past `provider_retry`, although the catalog usually holds another model that could have answered.
- **Now.** The settings key `model_fallback` (`models`: 1 to 8 model IDs of the catalog, any model, not only the profile's; `on_status` default 429, 500, 502, 503, 504, 529; `on_network_error` default true; `cooldown_ms` default 60000) wraps the run's provider client in `fallbackStreamer` (`app/model_fallback.go`). Each client keeps its own `provider_retry`, so the fallback is asked only after the session's model failed for good. It applies only when no event of the response reached the run: after `response.created` the run already holds part of that response, so a failure then is kept. A stopped run is never retried elsewhere. After the session's model failed, requests go to the fallbacks first for `cooldown_ms` (the runner, and so the client, is shared by the session's runs); when every fallback fails in that time, the session's model is still asked.
- **The request.** `rewriteForModel` keeps `input` (history and prompt) and changes the model name, the request defaults of the model file (the first model's are removed, the fallback's added) and the hosted tools at the end of `tools`. Fallback model files are loaded and their keys resolved when a run starts; one that cannot be used, or one that names the session's own model, is left out with a warning, so a missing spare key never stops the session's own model. Sub-agent runs use the session's list.
- **Not done.** No retry after part of a response arrived. No per-model health beyond the one cooldown. Reasoning items one provider wrote (encrypted reasoning) may be refused by another; a fallback on the same provider and `api` is the safe case, and a 400 it answers fails the run as before.
- **Tests.** `sessionfs/model_fallback_test.go` (defaults, values, refusals, model IDs resolved through the catalog with effort, unknown model and effort refused; the layout schema test covers the key), `app/model_fallback_test.go` (the next model on a listed status, then the cooldown and the return of the session's model; no fallback for an unlisted status, after an event, after a network error that followed an event, or for a stopped run; network errors with `on_network_error` on and off; the last failure returned and the session's model asked after the fallbacks in the cooldown; `rewriteForModel` both ways and with no tools; a headless run whose model answers 503 completed by a fallback on another server, a fallback without its key left out).
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged when the session's model answers. A fallback's response is committed like any other; its `response.model` is the fallback's.

## Run cost: model `pricing`, `cost_usd` and `max_run_cost_usd` (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** Runs, evals and sub-agents reported tokens but never money, and `max_run_tokens` cannot cap spend across models with different prices.
- **Now.** A model file may carry `pricing` (`input_per_mtok`, `output_per_mtok`, `cached_input_per_mtok` defaulting to the input price; US dollars per million tokens). `pricedStreamer` (`app/provider_cost.go`) wraps the provider client of a priced model and adds the cost of each final response event's usage (cached input at its price, the rest of the input and the output, reasoning included, at theirs) to the `session.CostMeter` of the run's context; every client gets its own, so a fallback's response is priced as that model. `Runner.Run` gives each run a meter that also adds into the meter of the context it started in. The HTTP executor puts one there and reports its total as `cost_usd` beside `usage` in the `usage` SSE event, once a priced response arrived; `jad run` returns it as `cost_usd` in its `json` and `jsonl` output, and `jad eval` sums it in its summary.
- **The cap.** The settings key `max_run_cost_usd` is checked between rounds like `max_run_tokens`: once the run's cost has reached it and the model asked for tool calls, the calls get a `run_cost_limit` result and the run fails with a message holding only the two amounts (shown to the client like the token-limit message). A run whose model file has no pricing is refused before the provider is asked (the reason is in the server log, like a missing key), and an unpriced fallback is left out.
- **Not counted.** *Superseded 2026-11-05 for sub-agents:* a child run's meter now adds into the calling run's (`SubagentRequest.CallerValues`, the calling tool call's context kept for its values; the child's cancellation still comes from its manager), so the caller's `max_run_cost_usd` and budget count the child's spend from the caller's next round on. The child's tokens still count only toward its own `max_run_tokens`. A background child that ends after the calling run adds into a meter nobody reads again, so the budget misses that part. Anthropic cache writes are priced as input tokens. Prices come from the model file and nothing checks them against the provider.
- **Tests.** `sessionfs/model_pricing_test.go` (values, default cached price, refusals, the cost formula, `max_run_cost_usd` refusals), `session/run_cost_test.go` (the meter adds into its parent, concurrently; the limit text), `app/run_cost_test.go` (a priced run with a tool round reports two responses' cost; an unpriced one reports none; a cap below one response stops the round with a `run_cost_limit` result in the transcript; a cap above lets it finish; a cap on an unpriced model fails before any provider request, and the test fails with the check removed), `evalrun/cost_test.go` (the summary sum).
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged without a cap. A capped run writes `run_cost_limit` tool results, the same shape as `run_token_limit`.

## The Anthropic adapter sends with `net/http` (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** The SDK was the transport only: the adapter built the Messages JSON and parsed every reply itself, and of the SDK it used `Messages.New` (for the raw JSON) and `Messages.NewStreaming` (for `Err()`, `Close()` and the raw body). Linking it cost 8.33 MB of `jad` (45,212,008 to 36,885,968 bytes, linux/amd64, `go build` of `cmd/jad` before and after) and 7.36 MB of `cmd/anthropic-adapter` (17,802,752 to 10,444,164). `go mod tidy` drops the SDK and six modules only it needed.
- **Now.** `transport.go`: `sendMessages` posts the adapter's payload once to `messagesURL(base_url)` with the `http.Client` the adapter was given (so `http_debug` and the OAuth transport still wrap it) and returns the response unread. The handlers map a status outside 2xx to `upstreamError` with at most `errorBodyLimit` bytes of the body, a non-SSE stream reply as before, and a failure with no answer to `callError` (502 `upstream_unreachable`, 504 when the request timeout ran out).
- **What changed upstream.** The request no longer carries the SDK's `User-Agent: Anthropic/Go 1.80.0` and its eight `X-Stainless-*` headers (recorded from the SDK build by `TestUpstreamRequestHeaders` run against it); it sends `User-Agent: jad-anthropic-adapter`, which `upstream.extra_headers` can replace, and `Accept: text/event-stream` for a stream (the SDK sent `application/json`). The URL is the SDK's: the base path is kept, with a slash added (`https://host/api` posts to `/api/v1/messages`). The non-streaming timeout is `upstream.request_timeout_ms` as before; the SDK's "streaming is required" refusal never applied, because that timeout was always set.
- **What changed for the caller.** A 200 with an empty body is `upstream_invalid_body` ("the upstream response is empty") and a 200 whose body is not JSON is `upstream_invalid_body` ("not valid JSON"); the SDK made both `upstream_read_failed`. The status, 502, is the same, so jad's retry rule sees no change. Error bodies, Anthropic's JSON or a gateway's text, keep their status and message as before.
- **Tests.** `transport_test.go`: the URL rule (5 base URLs and a real request under a path), the headers of both modes, a text error body in both modes, an empty and a broken 200 body, a body cut off after a 200 (`upstream_read_failed`), and the timeout (504). Run against the SDK build, 4 of these 6 pass; the headers test and the empty-body test fail, which are the two changes above. The adapter's other tests pass unchanged; `TestSDKDoesNotRetryNonStreamingFailures` is renamed `TestNonStreamingFailuresAreNotRetried`.
- **Not verified.** Nothing ran against the real Anthropic API, so whether Anthropic treats a subscription token differently without the SDK's headers is not known; the 2026-10-11 entry did not verify it with them either.

## `sandbox`: child processes of bash, exec, process and tool_smith in bubblewrap (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** `paths` lists and `tools.deny` are in-process limits: a shell command can write anywhere the backend's user can and reach the network. The container stays the security boundary (CLAUDE.md rule 0); `sandbox` adds a second one for the four tools that start programs the model wrote.
- **Now.** The settings key `sandbox` (session, profile or root `defaults`, like `model_fallback`). Off by default: absent, `null` or `enabled: false` loads as no sandbox and every child starts exactly as before. Enabled, `applySandbox` hands it to the settings of each tool in `tools` (default all four), and the three places a child starts (`runBashCommand` for bash, exec and tool_smith; `startStandby` for exec and tool_smith standbys; the process manager's `Start`) run `sandboxed()` between resolving the program and `exec.Command`. Hooks are covered only when `tools` lists `hooks` (follow-up below); glob's ripgrep, MCP servers and git checkpoints are not covered.
- **Backend `bwrap`** (default, Linux only): `--die-with-parent --unshare-all [--share-net] --ro-bind / / --dev /dev --proc /proc`, then `--bind-try` for each of `writable_paths` (default `{workspace}`, `{git_common_dir}`, `{tmp}`), `--tmpfs` (folder) or a `/dev/null` bind (file) for each existing `hide_paths` entry, `--chdir` the call's directory, and `--argv0` the configured name so `$0` is unchanged. `network: false` (default) leaves only loopback; the tool_smith bridge is a Unix socket under the temp folder, so it still works. `{git_common_dir}` is there for writer sub-agents: their worktree's git data lives in the repository's `.git`, outside `{workspace}`.
- **Backend `command`:** `command` (placeholders filled in) is put in front of the resolved program and its arguments, for `sandbox-exec`, firejail or a site wrapper. The child sees the resolved path as `$0`.
- **Fails closed.** An enabled sandbox whose program `exec.LookPath` cannot find, or backend `bwrap` off Linux, is a load error, so a session never runs without the sandbox it asked for. A program that disappears later fails the call with a spawn error. A standby's key includes the sandbox and workspace, so a standby is never shared across sandboxes.
- **Behaviour inside bwrap.** A command killed by a signal reports exit code 128+signal (bwrap's init exits with it) instead of the signal name. A timeout or stop signals the process group as before; `TestSandboxedBashTimeoutStopsTheSandbox` checks that nothing of the call is left running. Writes to `$HOME` (caches, `~/.npm`) fail unless a path under `{home}` is made writable. The covered tools' descriptions name the sandbox (follow-up below).
- **Tests.** `sessionfs/sandbox_test.go`: off by default, defaults and values, 16 refusals, which tools get it, loading through the layout, and the schema agreeing with the loader on 14 cases. `tools/native/sandbox_test.go`, with real bwrap (skipped where bwrap is missing or cannot make namespaces): bash writes the workspace and temp folder but not a folder outside them (`Read-only file system`), sees only `lo`, and keeps its exit code; hidden folder and file read empty; exec in python, fresh and from a standby, writes its result file and gets `EROFS` outside; tool_smith gets `EROFS` outside; a process cannot write outside; a timeout leaves nothing running. Without bwrap: the command backend wraps the child, the bwrap argument list, `gitCommonDir` for a checkout and a worktree, and the standby key.
- **Not verified.** bwrap was installed in the test container for this work (0.9.0); hosts where unprivileged user namespaces are blocked (some Docker and AppArmor setups) fail every sandboxed call with bwrap's own error. macOS `sandbox-exec` through backend `command` was not run. `messages.jsonl` is unchanged when the sandbox is off; with it on, tool results show what the sandbox refused, in the same shape.
- **Follow-up (2026-11-04): descriptions, hooks, `jad check`.**
  - *Descriptions.* `buildNativeTools` appends `sandboxDescription()` to the manifest description of each covered tool: bwrap lists the writable paths (placeholders as `<workspace>`, `<repository git directory>`, `<working directory>`; `{tmp}` and `{home}` as this machine's temp folder and `~`), the hidden paths, whether the network is on, and that a write elsewhere fails with "Read-only file system"; backend `command` names `command[0]`. Without a sandbox the description is the manifest's, byte for byte. tool_smith keeps "full interpreter privileges" (true of the interpreter; the sandbox sentence after it says where it runs), so its manifest copy stays equal to `toolSmithDescription`.
  - *Hooks.* `hooks` is a valid entry of `sandbox.tools` but not in the default list: hook commands come from the setting file, not the model, and checks such as `go test` write caches under `$HOME`. Listed, `applySandbox` sets `HooksSettings.Sandbox` and every hook command (`runHookChildPassed`) starts through `sandboxed()`. Changing the default would change running sessions that already enabled the sandbox.
  - *`jad check`.* `CheckLayout` collects each distinct enabled sandbox (by its JSON) with the first session or profile that uses it; `app.CheckFiles` starts `true` inside it once, from the session's working directory, with the same `sandboxed()` call a tool uses, and reports a failure as a session problem with the sandbox's first stderr line (for bwrap on a host without unprivileged user namespaces: bwrap's own message). New key `sandbox.check_timeout_ms` (default 10000, 100..600000) bounds that start; a timeout kills the process group. This is the only process `jad check` starts.
  - *Tests.* native: the description text for bwrap and command, the catalog adding it only when sandboxed, a sandboxed hook getting `Read-only file system` outside the workspace (real bwrap) and an unsandboxed one not, `ProbeSandbox` for no sandbox, a working and a failing wrapper, a hanging one (0.11 s with `check_timeout_ms` 100), a missing program and real bwrap. sessionfs: `hooks` and `check_timeout_ms` values and refusals, hooks left out by default and loaded when listed, `CheckLayout` collecting the sandbox, three more schema/loader cases. app: `CheckFiles` valid with a working wrapper and reporting the session with a failing one.

## `server.triggers`: scheduled and webhook-triggered runs (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** A run needed a client: the web UI, `jad run` or the remote API. Unattended work (a nightly test-and-fix pass, a run when CI fails or an issue opens) needed an outside cron and a script around `jad run`, and nothing could receive a webhook.
- **Now.** Root setting `server.triggers` (off by default): named `entries`, each with `session`, `prompt`, optional `profile`, `model`, `timeout_ms`, and a `schedule` (five-field cron, `timezone` default `Local`), a `webhook` (`secret_env`) or both. `internal/triggers.Manager` runs them in the server. Each firing sends the prompt (`{trigger}`, `{time}`, `{payload}` filled in once by one `strings.Replacer`, so a placeholder inside a payload stays literal) through `headless.Runner` to the API handler itself with the server's token: the same run route as the web client and `jad run`, so session locks, `messages.jsonl`, events and run limits are unchanged and the turn shows in the web client. `jad run` and `jad eval` turn triggers off (`NewHeadless`).
- **Webhooks.** `POST /api/triggers/<name>` sits in front of the API handler (`Manager.Wrap`), so it needs no API token; it needs the hex HMAC-SHA256 of the body in `signature_header` (default `X-Hub-Signature-256`, `sha256=` prefix optional, the GitHub form), compared with `hmac.Equal`. Unknown or schedule-only trigger 404, bad signature 401, body over `max_payload_bytes` 413, busy 409, started 202 with the run record. A webhook whose `secret_env` is unset or empty stops the server start, so no webhook is ever open. The payload is outside text that reaches the model; the schema says so.
- **Replay (decided 2026-11-05, impact trace C8).** The signature covered only the body, so anyone who captured one signed request could send it again and start another run, at any later time. Two settings, both off by default so existing senders keep working: `replay_window_ms` (0 to 86400000, default 0) keeps each accepted signature, by trigger, in memory for that long and answers a repeat 409 ("already accepted"); without a timestamp, the same body sent twice in the window is also refused, since its signature is the same. `timestamp_header` (unset by default; needs `replay_window_ms` above 0 and a name other than `signature_header`) makes every webhook carry the request time in Unix seconds and the signature cover `"<timestamp>.<body>"` (the Stripe form); a time more than the window from the server's clock is 401. With both, a captured request is refused inside the window by the record and after it by its time, so it can never run twice. The age is compared in whole seconds, so a far-off timestamp cannot overflow a duration. The record is pruned on each accepted request and holds only correctly signed ones, so only the senders' own traffic grows it; it is lost on restart, which `timestamp_header` covers. GitHub signs no timestamp, so a GitHub webhook can use `replay_window_ms` only (its redeliveries are new requests with the same body and are refused inside the window). A refused repeat starts no run and is not in the history; it is logged at warn.
- **Not behind CORS or the request log (kept, 2026-11-05).** `Manager.Wrap` runs before the API handler, so `/api/triggers*` skips `applyCORS` and `observeRequest`. CORS is not needed: a webhook comes from a server, not a page, the signature (or the API token for the list) is what admits it, and without CORS headers a page cannot read the answer; a preflight to the list gets 405. The manager logs its own refusals (warn), starts, skips and ends, so the request log missing these routes hides no outcome.
- **One run per trigger.** A firing while the trigger's previous run goes on is recorded as `skipped` (webhook 409) instead of queueing; a run the route refuses (unknown session, a run already active on the session) is `refused` with the route's message.
- **Status.** `GET /api/triggers` (API token, constant-time compare) lists the triggers with their next scheduled time and the run in progress, and the last `history_limit` runs newest first (in memory, not kept across restarts). Starts, ends and skips are logged.
- **Shutdown.** `Service.Close` closes the manager first: the schedulers stop and the runs in progress are stopped through the stop route (their context is cancelled), then the API closes.
- **Cron.** `internal/cronexpr`, no dependency: `*`, numbers, ranges, steps, lists, 7 as Sunday, the `@` macros, cron's OR rule when both day fields are restricted; `Next` skips by month, day and hour and gives up after five years (for example `0 0 30 2 *`). No month or day names. Time zones need the system's zoneinfo (`time/tzdata` is not embedded, to keep the binary size).
- **Tests.** `cronexpr` (16 next-times including Feb 29 and the OR rule, location kept, never-matching, 13 refusals). `triggers` with a fake runner and clock (`-race`, three runs): signed webhook passes session, profile, model, timeout and the filled prompt; payload not re-expanded; refusals (no, wrong and non-hex signature, other body, unknown and schedule-only trigger, 413, wrong method, list without or with a wrong token) start nothing; busy firings are skipped and the history is bounded; a refused run is recorded; the scheduler waits 90 minutes from 00:00 UTC for `30 3 * * *` at UTC+2, fires, and `Close` aborts its run; a missing secret fails `New`. sessionfs: defaults, values, 21 refusals, 17 schema/loader cases. app: a signed webhook runs the session on the real service (the provider gets the prompt with the payload and the trigger's model; the list shows `completed`), unsigned and anonymous calls get 401, and a missing secret stops `NewService` but not `NewHeadless`.
- **Not covered.** No retry, catch-up of missed schedules while the server was down, persistence of the history, or per-trigger concurrency above one. No live server was started for this work; the app test drives the real handler through `httptest`.

## `notebook`: Jupyter notebook cells as a native tool (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** A notebook is JSON with sources as lists of lines and outputs inline (base64 images included), so read showed noise and edit needed exact JSON fragments, escapes and all; one slip broke the file.
- **Now.** `tools.native.notebook` (manifest `sample/tools/native/notebook.json`) with `action` read, edit, insert or delete, a cell picked by `cell` (index) or `cell_id`. read shows each cell's index, id, type, execution count and source, and its outputs as text: streams, `text/plain` results, errors with ANSI removed, each cut to `max_output_chars` (default 2000, 0 hides output text); images and other MIME types are named with their size only. edit replaces the source and can change the type; editing a code cell's source clears its outputs and execution count (`outputs_cleared: true`). insert adds a cell (code by default) before `cell` or at the end, with a random id from nbformat 4.5 on. Nothing is executed. Only nbformat 4.
- **File layout.** The file is decoded with `UseNumber` and written back as Jupyter does (keys sorted, one-space indent, non-ASCII as is, HTML characters unescaped, final newline, sources as lists of lines), so a diff shows only the changed cell. Checked against nbformat 5.10 in a scratch install: a notebook `nbformat.write` produced, edited by the tool, validates, `nbformat.write` of the result is byte-identical, and the diff touches only the edited cell (metadata `1.25e-7` and a 20-digit integer kept).
- **Permissions.** No path settings of its own: it needs `tools.native.edit` and takes its path rules, `max_bytes` and backup settings, through the same path locks, so a profile that may edit a file may edit the notebook (reads use the same rules). It is not `ReadOnly`: a read-only sub-agent gets every `ReadOnly` tool and this one writes; it is in `SubagentChangeTools`, so a writer agent may list it (worktree path scope applies). Checkpoints count every action but read as a change. `post_edit` hooks run on a changed notebook (wrapped like edit). It takes a leaf-budget slot.
- **Schemas.** `setting.schema.json` (`tools.native.notebook.max_output_chars`), the three tool-name patterns of `profile.schema.json`, the leaf-tool list in `root-setting.schema.json`.
- **Tests.** native: read (cells, outputs cut and counted, image named, traceback without ANSI, a range, output hidden at 0, every output ending its line); an edit's file equal byte for byte to the Jupyter layout with only the cell changed; insert, delete, type changes and ids; no ids before 4.5 with the exact file; 7 refusals that leave the file alone and 4 files that are not notebooks; not read-only; the sample manifest loads and its schema equals the code's. sessionfs: defaults from edit, refusals, needs edit, schema/loader agreement, loading through a session. checkpoint: edit, insert and delete count, read does not.
- **messages.jsonl.** Unchanged unless the tool is enabled; its results use the usual result envelope.

## Prompt caching on by default for in-process Anthropic models; cache writes priced (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** The adapter could place cache breakpoints (`anthropic.cache`: tools, system, history, automatic, ttl) but every switch was off unless the model file set it, so a jad run resent its whole prefix at the full input price every round. An agent run is the case caching is made for: the same tools, instructions and growing history, seconds apart. And with caching on, the run cost (`pricing`) billed cache writes at the input price, though Anthropic charges 1.25x (5 minutes) and 2x (1 hour).
- **Now.** `anthropicadapter.ParseEmbeddedConfig` (models with `api: anthropic_messages`) uses `EmbeddedCacheDefault()`, `{tools, system, history}` with the 5-minute lifetime, when the model file's `anthropic` object has no `cache` key or is absent. A `cache` object replaces the default as a whole (decoded onto an empty `CacheConfig`), so `{}` turns caching off and `{"automatic": true}` gives only the automatic breakpoint. The standalone `anthropic-adapter` keeps everything off (`DefaultConfig`): its callers are unknown. History, not automatic: jad inserts state context as messages after conversation items, the adapter folds them into unmarked text, so the breakpoint stays on the last real block and a changed state block does not move it. At most three of Anthropic's four breakpoints are used. A prompt below the model's minimum cacheable length is simply not cached.
- **Pricing.** `pricing.cache_write_per_mtok` (default `input_per_mtok`) and `cache_write_1h_per_mtok` (default `cache_write_per_mtok`). `ModelPricing.Cost(TokenUsage)` takes cached, 5-minute and 1-hour writes out of `input_tokens` in that order (each clamped to what is left), prices the rest at the input price; `CostUSD` is `Cost` without writes. `pricedStreamer` reads `input_tokens_details.cache_creation_5m_tokens` / `cache_creation_1h_tokens` from the translation, and counts a `cache_creation_tokens` total the split does not cover as 5-minute writes. Providers that report no writes (Responses, Chat Completions) cost as before. Defaults keep an existing pricing block's numbers unchanged.
- **messages.jsonl.** The format is unchanged: usage items already carried `input_tokens_details` from the translation. Values differ for an in-process Anthropic model that now caches (`cached_tokens` above 0), as they would with the setting turned on by hand. `api: responses` models are untouched.
- **Tests.** anthropicadapter: absent, `null` and a `cache`-less object give the tools, system and last-history breakpoints on the cache fixture (`tool1,system1,msg2.0`); `{}` none; `{"automatic":true}` and `{"tools":true,"ttl":"1h"}` only theirs; `DefaultConfig().Cache` still empty. sessionfs: the new price keys, their defaults and a refusal; `Cost` with all five parts and with parts larger than the input. app: `responseCost` with the split, with a total only and with no writes; the in-process Anthropic tool round now checks that the second upstream request marks the last tool and the tool result and has at most four breakpoints.
- **Not proven.** No real Anthropic call was made, so the hit rate and saving on a real run were not measured.

## `lsp`: code intelligence from language servers (Go only, 2026-11-04)

- **Why.** The model found definitions and uses with grep and learned about type errors only by running a build: slow, and wrong for overloaded names, methods, re-exports and anything spread over packages.
- **Now.** `tools.native.lsp` (manifest `sample/tools/native/lsp.json`) with `servers` by name (`command`, `extensions`, `language_id`, optional `initialization_options`, `env`) and actions diagnostics, definition, references, hover, type_definition, implementation, symbols and workspace_symbols. A position is `line` (1-based) and `column` (1-based, in characters) or `symbol` (its text on the line); characters are converted to LSP's UTF-16 offsets and back. Results are `path:line:column` relative to the workspace with the line's text; locations a server returns as `LocationLink` use the name's range. `max_results` cuts long lists and reports the full count.
- **Servers.** `internal/tools/native/lsp_client.go`: JSON-RPC 2.0 over stdio with `Content-Length` framing; one reader goroutine routes answers, stores `publishDiagnostics` and answers the server's own requests (`workspace/configuration` with one `null` per item, anything else with `null`) so a server never waits on jad. A process-wide pool keeps one server per server setting and workspace root (a worktree sub-agent gets its own), started on the first call with `initialize`/`initialized` within `request_timeout_ms`, kept across runs (catalogs are rebuilt per run), and stopped after `idle_shutdown_ms` without a call (`shutdown`, `exit`, then the process group is killed after the request timeout).
