- **Text.** The default `plan_mode.instructions` now says exec runs only calls with `read_only` true and that calls that change files are not available; it is sent with each request of a plan-mode run and never stored, so `messages.jsonl` is unchanged. The plan-mode refusal text is unchanged.
- **Tests.** `TestExecCallChangesStateUnlessItIsReadOnlyOrAListing`, `TestPlanModeRunsReadOnlyExecCallsAndRefusesTheOthers`, `TestExecReadOnlyCallStartsInThePlanSandbox`, `TestExecReadOnlyCallCannotWriteTheProjectUnderBwrap` (skipped without bwrap), `TestSubagentWithoutAToolsListDoesNotGetExec`, `TestExecDefinitionFileWithoutReadOnlyStillLoads`, `TestReadOnlyAgentForcesReadOnlyExecCalls`, `TestNativeSubagentReadOnlyAgentIsNotAWriter`, `TestNativeSubagentReadOnlyRefusals`.

## `exec` `read_only` follow-ups: plan-mode checkpoints, `read_only_calls`, read-only standbys (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

A trace of the `read_only` change found these; the user asked for all four to be fixed.

- **No checkpoint for plan-mode guarded calls.** `Catalog.PlanMode` marks the tools it wraps with `planModeGuard` (`Tool.PlanGuarded`), and `roundChangesWorkspace` skips their calls: a call that could change files is refused before it runs, and the others change none. Before, any `exec` call of a plan-mode turn (a read-only one or a refused one) and a refused call to a writer agent took the checkpoint, because the checkpoint counts `exec` by name and runs before the refusal; plan turns then took snapshots and checkpoint slots (`max_checkpoints`). A tool that `plan_mode.allow_tools` keeps whole is not guarded and still counts, and outside plan mode every `exec` call still takes the checkpoint. So in plan mode with no sandbox and `read_only_calls: "trusted"`, a broken `read_only` promise is not snapshotted, as for any other read-only tool there; `"sandboxed"` refuses such calls instead.
- **`tools.native.exec.read_only_calls`** (`"trusted"`, the default, or `"sandboxed"`). `trusted` keeps the behaviour above. `sandboxed` honors `read_only` only where a bwrap or seatbelt sandbox covers exec (`NativeExecSettings.ReadOnlyHonored`); elsewhere a call with `read_only` counts as one that may change files: it runs in order and plan mode refuses it (the call still runs outside plan mode, with the configured sandbox if any). The default stays `trusted` so plan mode can read the workspace without a sandbox. An agent with `read_only: true` is refused at load where its exec does not honor `read_only` (the session's exec, or the exec of the toolset it names, checked once at the end of `loadAgentToolsets`, for the session's own agents only): it would be scheduled as a reader while each of its exec calls counts as a change. The agents a toolset itself defines are not checked, since an agent using that toolset drops them (`TestReadOnlyCheckSkipsTheAgentsOfAnAgentsToolset`), and a toolset file is not checked when it is saved: the sandbox is a session or profile setting (a toolset carries none), so the scratch session that check builds never has one and would refuse every `sandboxed` toolset with a `read_only` agent, even one used only in sandboxed sessions. A profile using it is checked when its session loads, where the sandbox is known. The exec schema text was left unchanged here; the next-but-one section changes it.
- **Read-only standbys under a sandbox.** A read-only call starts in the plan-mode sandbox, so its standby key differs and it could not use a standby that a normal call warmed. A call outside plan mode under bwrap or seatbelt now also refills the read-only form's key, after its own, within the same `standby_pool` and `standby_max`. A plan-mode call does not warm the other way. No new setting: `standby_pool: 0` still turns standby off.
- **Doc comment.** `withReadOnlyExec`'s comment had been put inside `subagentChildTools`'s; they are separate again, and `subagentChildTools`'s names the opt-in tools.
- **Tests.** `TestRoundChangesWorkspaceSkipsPlanGuardedCalls`, `TestExecStandbyWarmsTheReadOnlyFormUnderASandbox`, `TestExecReadOnlyCallsSandboxedNeedsAnEnforcingSandbox`, `TestExecReadOnlyCallsIsReadWithTrustedAsTheDefault`, `TestExecReadOnlyHonoredOnlyUnderAnEnforcingSandboxWhenSandboxed`, `TestReadOnlyAgentIsRefusedWhereReadOnlyIsNotHonored`; each fails without its fix.

## Three races behind flaky tests; the checkpoint list loses the removed tools (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

- **A handle that finishes during a delete or reset.** `asyncmgr.Manager.Complete` writes the result record between two locked sections and marks the row `finishing` meanwhile. `cancel` and `DeleteOwnersWithPrefix` skipped finishing rows, and `Complete` used the end action it read before the write, so a handle that ended while its session was deleted kept an unread signal under the deleted owner (and a reset left it unread too). `cancel` now raises the end action of a finishing row as well (it does not stop it again or count it), and `Complete` reads the end action again under its second lock: a removal removes the row and its record, a born-read end clears the record's unread flag on disk. The same holds at the end of a sub-agent run (`DeleteOwnersWithPrefix(run.id)`): a child's handle that ended just then stayed as an unread signal under the finished run until its TTL. The counts these calls return are unchanged: a finishing handle is still not counted as stopped. This was `TestSharedStorageSessionDeleteStopsItsProcessesAndLeavesOtherSessionsAlone` failing about 1 run in 20 under load. Tests: `TestDeleteWhileAHandleIsFinishingRemovesIt`, `TestResetWhileAHandleIsFinishingMakesItsEndRead` (through the test-only hook `beforeCompleteCommit`); both fail without the fix.
- **A request cancelled before the in-process status.** A handler that returns because its request was cancelled sends its 200 as it ends, so `inprocess.Transport.RoundTrip`'s select could pick the status and return a response with no error. It now returns the context's error whenever the request is cancelled once the select ends. This was `TestTransportCancelledBeforeTheStatus` failing about 1 run in 2,000 with 4 CPUs.
- **The checkpoint list.** `checkpoint.CallChangesWorkspace` still named `write`, `edit`, `apply_patch`, `bash`, `notebook` and `process`, which the loader now refuses by name. It counts `exec` (every call) and MCP tools only; the rest is the tool's own `MayChangeFiles`. No tool can carry those names any more (every catalog tool is a fixed-name native or extension tool, or MCP, which is counted before the name check), so the only change is that a call to one of them is a call to an unknown tool and no longer takes a checkpoint.
- **Not fixed here** (fixed in the next section). `asyncmgr`'s test fixture never closes its manager, so a delayed index write can race the TempDir cleanup ("directory not empty"; seen once in 8 package runs, with and without these changes).

## Follow-ups of the flaky-test fixes: shutdown during a reset, a panic with a cancel, the exec `read_only` text (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

- **The `asyncmgr` test fixture closes its managers.** `fixture.open` registers a cleanup that closes every manager it opens (`Close` is idempotent; `t.TempDir`'s cleanup runs after it), so no delayed index write lands in the folder while it is removed. During that close the fake driver reports each stop at once (`fakeDriver.ending`), so the close does not wait `StopWait` for the handles a test left live; a `Close` inside a test behaves as before. Measured: the unchanged fixture failed 1 of 20 package runs ("directory not empty" in `TestListPaginationAndRetentionAt1kHandles`), the new one 0 of 30; the package run time stayed within the same noisy range (3.3 to 5.8 s before, 4.6 to 6.0 s after).
- **A shutdown while a reset or deleted handle's end is recorded.** `Complete` wrote the result record (unread), then committed the end, then rewrote or removed the record when a reset or delete had come meanwhile. A `Close` that fell between, after its `StopWait` gave up on the write, shut file writes first, so the record stayed unread on disk (or a deleted session's record stayed) and the next start could queue its signal again. `Complete` now holds `ioMu` from the result write until the record matches the committed end (the late rewrite for a reset, the removal for a delete, also when the delete came before the claim), and `Close` shuts file writes under `ioMu`, so it waits for that. Other file writes (index rewrites, other records) wait for this commit too. The test-only hook `beforeCompleteCommit` now runs with `ioMu` held, so the two tests that reset or delete from it start that call on a goroutine and wait until it has marked the row. Test: `TestCloseWhileAResetOrDeletedHandleEndsLeavesNoUnreadRecord` (reset and delete, `StopWait` 0); it failed 5 of 5 runs without the change. Not changed here (fixed in the next section): a `Close` that shuts file writes before the result write leaves the record running, and the handle is lost at the next start, like any handle that did not report in time.
- **A handler that panics while its request is cancelled.** `inprocess.Transport.RoundTrip` returns the cancel when the request is cancelled; when the handler had already panicked before its status, the error now carries both (`context canceled; in-process handler panicked: ...`, `errors.Is` holds for both). A cancel that comes before the panic still reports the cancel alone, since there is no panic yet (the next section logs it). Test: `TestTransportCancelledWhileTheHandlerPanicsKeepsBoth` (a context that reports the cancel without closing `Done`, so both are ready at once); it fails without the change.
- **No check at toolset save.** Considered and left out: see the corrected `read_only_calls` entry above (a toolset has no sandbox, so a save-time check cannot know whether `read_only` is honored).
- **The exec `read_only` text.** It ended "without one, read_only is your promise not to change files", which is wrong where `read_only_calls` is `sandboxed` and there is no sandbox. It now goes on "and a session may be set to ignore it: the call then runs in order and plan mode refuses it". `requireExecDefinition` also accepts a definition file with the previous text (`execInputSchemaPromiseOnly`), as it accepts the one from before `read_only`, and shows the model the current schema. `sample/tools/native/exec.json` has the new text, and `TestProfileTranscriptsAndExecManifestsMatchTheGoSchemas` pins it (the catalog alone would accept the old one). `messages.jsonl` holds no tool schemas, so it is unaffected. **Restart:** a backend still running the old binary refuses the new `exec.json`, so restart it once this binary and file are in place.
- **Docs.** `CLAUDE.md` no longer says an agent gets `write`, `edit`, `bash` or `process` from its `tools` list (they are refused by name), and `design_checkpoint.md` section 4 names what takes a checkpoint now: any `exec` call, a call whose tool's `MayChangeFiles` is true, and MCP tools unless `include_mcp_tools` is off.

## The three open items of the follow-ups: a busy target right after a reply, a panic after a cancel, handles left running at shutdown (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

- **`profile_message` ends once the target is free.** A non-queued message ended on the target run's `Done`, which closes in `finish`, before `executeRun`'s deferred steps (the async-state watcher, the "run finished" log, the span) and before the transcript lock is released. A message sent right after the reply could find the target "busy" (`TestProfileMessageNonQueuedLeavesNoGoroutinesAfterCompletion` failed so once under the full suite). The message now also waits for the run's `OnEnd` hook, which httpapi calls after the lock is released (as `remote_api` already does), so its outcome, and an async handle's signal, come once the target can take the next message. Every path out of `executeRun` ends the run, so `OnEnd` always comes. Test: `TestProfileMessageEndsOnlyOnceTheTargetIsFree`, whose logger pauses 100 ms on "run finished" to hold the gap open; it fails 5 of 5 without the change. The layout fixture takes optional config changes for it (`newLayoutFixtureWithBudget`).
- **A panic after the cancel is logged.** `inprocess.Transport` has a `Logger`; every handler panic is logged at error level with its stack, method, path and whether the handler was cancelled (its own context, which the request's cancel and a closed response body both end; at first it read only the request's, so a panic after the caller closed the body logged `cancelled=false`). The resolver passes its logger. A panic after `RoundTrip` returned the cancel reaches no caller, and the error a caller gets never had the stack. Tests: `TestTransportLogsAPanicAfterTheCancel`, which fails without the log, and `TestTransportLogsAPanicAfterTheBodyIsClosedAsCancelled`, which fails with the request's context.
- **`Close` ends the handles that did not report.** After its `StopWait`, `Close` ends each live handle this server registered that is not being finished as `lost` with reason `server_shutdown`, through `Complete` (unread unless a reset or delete came first, pruning and the index as usual), and only then shuts file writes. Left `running`, such a record was lost only at the next start, and a server started while this process was still alive took it for this one's live handle (`foreignLive`) and kept it live. A driver that reports later gets `false` from `Complete`, as for any handle already ended. Handles another live server owns (record `server` not this one) are left as they are. A handle whose `Complete` already claimed it when `StopWait` ran out is skipped, since its result is on its way (the next section makes `Close` wait for it). The tail of `Close` that writes the final index and stops writes is `shutFiles`; the tests that stood a `Close` in for a server that died, or one still running elsewhere, now use `fixture.abandon`, which shuts the files without ending handles. Tests: `TestCloseEndsAnUnreportedHandleAsLost` (fails without the change) and `TestCloseLeavesALiveForeignServersHandleRunning` (fails when the ownership check is removed).

## Shutdown gaps of the async manager: a claimed result, a change cut by Close, shell stops past the wait, a stale final index (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

- **`Close` waits for a `Complete` that already claimed its handle.** Once the stop wait ran out and the unreported handles are ended as lost, `Close` waits, without a limit, until no `Complete` is past its claim (`completing` is 0), and only then writes the final index and shuts file writes. Such a `Complete` has only its own files left to write, no driver work. Before, a claim made before the wait ran out could write its result after file writes stopped: the write was skipped and the record stayed `running`. After that loop no `Complete` can claim a handle of this server. Test: `TestCloseWaitsForACompleteThatClaimedItsHandle` (a hook, `beforeCompleteWrite`, holds the `Complete` between its claim and its write); it fails 3 of 3 without the wait.
- **A change holds the file lock until its files are written.** `commit(index, change)` takes `ioMu`, makes the in-memory change under `mu`, writes its record work (`applyWorkIOLocked`) and only then releases `ioMu`. `DropUnread` (the read half of a reset), `MarkRead`, `Prune`, `DeleteOwnersWithPrefix` (the finished handles it removes) and the prune at the start of `Wait` use it; a `Wait` that delivers signals holds `ioMu` from the pop until they are marked read on disk; `Register` holds it from the new row until its record is written; `Complete` now does its prune work before releasing it. `Close` shuts file writes under `ioMu`, so it finds each change either on disk or not made. Before, a reset, a read or a delivery cut by `Close` changed only memory: after a restart the signals came back unread, and a delivered one could be delivered again; a deleted record stayed. Lock order is now `indexMu`, `ioMu`, `mu`. A `Wait` round takes `ioMu`, so it waits behind a record write in progress. Test: `TestCloseDuringAChangeFindsItOnDisk` (drop unread, mark read, wait delivery, delete; a hook, `beforeCommitWrite`, runs `Close` between the change and its files); every case fails when the change releases `ioMu` before its files. `TestCloseWhileAResetOrDeletedHandleEndsLeavesNoUnreadRecord` now waits for `Close` to start rather than for its final index, since `Close` waits for that `Complete` first.
- **The final index matches the records.** `Close` reads the rows for its last `index.json` under `ioMu` (after taking `indexMu`, so a coalesced writer finishes first) and sets `ioClosed` in the same hold. Before, it read the rows before taking `ioMu`: a `Complete` that committed meanwhile could leave an index older than its records, such as a row for a handle whose record a delete removed (one `async record unreadable; dropped` warning at the next start). With the wait above and `commit`, every change is either in that index and on disk, or in neither. The old window was too narrow to reproduce on demand; the reset/delete and `commit` tests now also check the final index.
- **A shutdown waits for shell stops.** `AsyncHost.Close` passes the longest stop bound of its live shell runs to `Manager.CloseWithin`, which waits up to the longer of that and `stop_wait_ms`; the wait still ends as soon as every handle has reported. The bound is `shellStopBound`: `kill_grace_ms` + `kill_deadline_ms` + `drain_after_exit_ms` of the run's child settings (the child's own defaults for a zero deadline or drain), the longest a stopped child can take before `bash_child` gives up on it. Before, `Close` waited only `stop_wait_ms` (default 2000, as is `kill_grace_ms`), so a child that ignored SIGTERM was killed after the wait and its handle ended `lost` instead of `stopped`; `waitShellRuns`, which runs after the manager is closed, could only wait for the goroutine. A shutdown can now take up to the longest such bound (at most 3 minutes with the 60-second caps). `stop_wait_ms`'s doc and schema description say so. Tests: `TestAsyncHostCloseWaitsForAStubbornRun` now reopens the folder and wants the handle `stopped` with `server_shutdown` (it was `lost` before the change), and `TestShellStopBoundAddsTheGraceDeadlineAndDrain`.

## Shutdown waits only for this server's handles, and closes async hosts together (Go, 2026-11-06)

Found while tracing the change above, which lengthened the shutdown wait.

- **`Close` stops and waits for only this server's live handles.** `CloseWithin` takes the own live handles (`ownLiveLocked`, the set `unreportedOwnLive` already used: no `Server` in the record, or this server's), stops those that have a driver, and waits until each of them has ended and no `Complete` is past its claim. Before, it stopped every live row, including one another live server owns in the same folder (kept live at `Open` by `foreignLive`), and waited on `liveTotal`, which counts that row. A foreign handle never reports here, so `Close` always waited the whole `max(stop_wait_ms, longest shell stop)`: up to 3 minutes since the change above. A `Stop` call on a foreign handle did nothing in production drivers, which know only their own runs. Test: `TestCloseDoesNotWaitForALiveForeignServersHandle`; it took 20 s, the whole wait, before the fix.
- **`Resolver.Close` closes its async hosts together.** There is one host per tool storage folder, and each may now wait up to its longest shell stop, so closing them one after another made a shutdown wait for the sum. They are separate folders and managers, so they now close in parallel and the shutdown waits for the slowest. The order around them is kept: remote followers and sub-agent managers before them, MCP managers after them. Test: `TestResolverClosesAsyncHostsTogether` (three hosts, each with a handle whose driver never reports and an 800 ms stop wait); it took 2.4 s before the fix.

## Open gaps after the shutdown changes: a swallowed second signal, a late-built host, a dead peer's handles (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

The user chose to fix these three of the open gaps listed after the change above. Whether `ShutdownTimeout` should also cap the async shutdown wait was decided afterwards: it does not (below).

- **A second interrupt or SIGTERM ends the process.** `run` took its signals with `signal.NotifyContext` and reset them only when it returned, so during a shutdown that waits for the async handles (up to the longest shell stop) every later signal was caught and dropped. `notifyShutdown` resets the handlers as soon as the first signal arrives (`context.AfterFunc`), so the default action of a second one ends the process. Test: `TestSecondSignalEndsTheProcessDuringShutdown` (a child process); before the fix the repeated SIGINTs were ignored for the 5 s it allows.
- **A host or sub-agent manager built after `Resolver.Close` is closed before its build is marked done.** `Close` waits for the pending builds, but the builder marked its build done and only then closed what it built, so `Close` could return while the host still ended its handles and wrote its final index. The builder now closes first. Test: `TestResolverCloseWaitsForAHostBuiltAfterIt` (a host with a handle whose driver never reports, 400 ms stop wait); before the fix the handle was still running when `Close` returned. The sub-agent path got the same reordering; a freshly built sub-agent manager has no runs, so that half has no test of its own.
- **A handle kept live for another server ends as lost once that server stops.** `Open` keeps another live server's running handles as they are (`foreignLive`), but nothing looked at them again, so when that server died they showed running, counted against `max_handles` and `max_handles_total`, and held waits until this server restarted. `Open` now remembers them with their server, and `List`, `Status`, `Stop`, `Wait` and `Register` first check each such server once (`endDeadForeign`, outside the locks) and end the handles of a stopped one through `Complete`: `lost`, reason `backend_restarted`, unread, as the next start would. With no foreign handle live the check is one atomic load; `Counts` and `Snapshot` do not check, so a header can show such a handle until the next of those calls. There is still no goroutine, so a handle nobody reads ends at the next start, as before. Test: `TestAForeignHandleEndsAsLostWhenItsServerStops`; before the fix the handle stayed running after its server stopped.

## `shutdown_timeout_ms` does not cap the async shutdown wait; the hosts-together test no longer times (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

- **The async wait keeps its own bounds.** `service.Close` stops this server's async handles and waits up to `max(stop_wait_ms, longest shell stop)` (kill grace + kill deadline + drain) before `Server.Shutdown` runs, and `server.shutdown_timeout_ms` limits only that HTTP part. The user chose to keep this and document it rather than cap the whole shutdown or add a separate cap: the wait is already bounded by the settings that make a killed child record its stop, a second SIGINT or SIGTERM ends it (see above), and exiting earlier could leave a shell child's process group running until the next start sweeps it. No behaviour change; the setting's comment, its schema description and design §9.7 say so.
- **`TestResolverClosesAsyncHostsTogether` checks overlap, not time.** It failed when `Close` took over 1.6 s for three hosts that each wait 800 ms, which a loaded machine could reach. Each host's handle now has a driver whose stop is held until the test has seen the stops of all three hosts; only a `Close` that closes them together gets there. Closing the hosts one after another makes it time out on the second stop.

## A stopped peer's handle keeps the end that peer recorded (Go, 2026-11-06)

Found while tracing the change that ends a dead peer's handles. `endDeadForeign` ended every such handle through `Complete`, which writes the record from the row this server read at `Open`. When the peer had ended the handle itself before it stopped (its result, its `server_shutdown` stop at a clean exit, or a delete), that end was overwritten with `lost` / `backend_restarted`: the peer's result and summary were gone, and a deleted record was written again. Before that change the row only stayed `running` until this server restarted, which then read the peer's end from disk. `endForeign` now reads the record again first. A terminal record is taken as it is (state, unread mark), a missing one drops the row, and only a live or unreadable record still ends as `lost` through `Complete`. A reset or delete this server asked for meanwhile still applies: a reset's end is read, a delete's record is removed. Tests: `TestAForeignHandleKeepsTheEndItsServerWrote` (before the fix the done handle came back `lost` with an empty summary) and `TestAForeignHandlesRecordedEndKeepsAResetOrDelete`.

## A stopped peer's unreadable record is not overwritten; a live peer's handle cannot be stopped here (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

The user chose these two of the open gaps listed after the change above.

- **An unreadable record drops the handle and is left as it is.** `endForeign` still ended a handle whose record it could not read through `Complete`, which wrote `lost` from the row read at `Open`. The likely cause is the peer restarting as a newer build that rewrote the record in another version, so this server overwrote a record the newer one could read with one it could not, and the newer server then dropped it at its next start. It now drops the row without writing, as `recover` does with an unreadable record, and logs a warning; a delete this server asked for meanwhile still removes the file. This supersedes "or unreadable" in the section above. Test: `TestAForeignHandlesUnreadableRecordIsDroppedNotOverwritten`; before the fix the handle came back `lost` and the record was overwritten.
- **`Stop` of a live handle another running server owns is refused at once.** Every production driver (shell, sub-agent, profile message, remote sub-agent) knows only its own runs, so such a stop did nothing, waited out `stop_wait_ms` and reported the handle still running. `Stop` now returns `ErrForeignHandle` (naming the server's pid) without calling the driver, and the `async` tool answers `handle_foreign` (blocked) with the id. A dead server's handles are ended first (`endDeadForeign`), so a stop after that server stopped still returns their end. `Status`, `wait`, `list` and the stops of a reset or delete (`cancel`) are unchanged. Tests: `TestStopRefusesALiveForeignServersHandle` and `TestAsyncToolStopRefusesAnotherServersHandle`; before the fix both waited the 10 s stop wait and got a running entry.

## A signal another server delivered is not delivered again; a stopped peer's handles end without a call (Go, 2026-11-06; decided by the user)

The last open gaps of two servers sharing one tool storage folder.

- **A wait leaves out a signal whose record is already read.** A server that starts while another runs loads the other's unread signals, and both servers load the `lost` end of a stopped peer's handle (the restarted peer at its start, this one in `endForeign`). Each kept its unread mark in memory, so a session talking to both servers got the signal twice. A wait's delivery already reads each record to clear its mark; when that read finds the record terminal and already read, the signal is left out of the result and nothing is written (the row is read in memory, the index is rewritten). A wait whose popped signals were all left out looks again, and waits on when nothing else is ready. A record that is still live (its result write failed) or cannot be read is delivered from memory as before. No extra file read; two deliveries at the same moment can still both return the signal, since the servers share no lock. Test: `TestASignalAnotherServerDeliveredIsNotDeliveredAgain`; before the fix the earlier server delivered the signal the later one had.
- **A background check ends a stopped peer's handles (`tools.native.async.peer_check_ms`, default 5000, 0–21600000).** While handles of another live server are kept, a timer (`time.AfterFunc`, like the delayed index write; armed at `Open` only when there are such handles and stopped by `Close`, which waits for a running check before its final index) runs `endDeadForeign` every `peer_check_ms` and stops once none is left. A wait in progress is woken by the end, and the run stream's watcher is told. 0 turns it off. `Counts` and `SnapshotLive` (the run stream's counts and ids) now run the check too. This supersedes "`Counts` and `Snapshot` do not check" and "a handle nobody reads ends at the next start" in the 2026-11-06 open-gaps section. Tests: `TestAWaitInProgressSeesAPeerStop` (before the fix the wait did not return within 3 s), `TestCountsAndSnapshotEndAStoppedPeersHandle` (the counts still showed the handle live), `TestClosedManagerRunsNoPeerCheck` (a guard: a closed manager writes nothing), and the config tests.
- **The overflow mark a stopped peer recorded is kept.** `endForeign` took the end, state and unread mark from the peer's record but not its `overflow` mark (the signal was dropped to the unread cap), so the index lost it. It is now copied. Test: `TestAForeignHandleKeepsTheOverflowMarkItsServerRecorded`.

## A wait that skips a signal another server delivered keeps the dropped count (Go, 2026-11-06)

Found while tracing the change above. `Wait` took the owner's dropped count (signals lost to the unread cap or TTL) in the same pass as the pop, before `deliverIOLocked` knew whether any popped signal was left. When every popped signal had been delivered by another server, the wait went on waiting with the count already cleared; if it then ended in a cancellation or `ErrClosed`, the count was never reported to the model. The count is now taken only when the wait returns a result (`takeDropped`), under the same rule as before: with signals, with nothing pending, or at the timeout. A drop that lands between the pop and that moment is reported in this result rather than the next one. Single-server results are otherwise unchanged. Test: `TestAWaitThatSkipsADeliveredSignalKeepsTheDroppedCount`; before the fix `list` after the cancelled wait showed dropped 0 instead of 2.

## The peer check takes the ends a running peer recorded (Go, 2026-11-06)

An open gap from the tracing of the dropped-count fix. A handle kept live at `Open` for another server that is still running reports its end only to that server: this one saw it only once that server stopped. Until then it showed `running` here, counted against `max_handles` and `max_handles_total`, kept waits waiting (`Pending` > 0), and a `stop` of it answered `handle_foreign`.

- **The background check reads those records again.** `peerCheck` now runs `checkForeign(true)`: for a server still running it reads each of its handles' records and, through `endForeign(id, alive)`, takes a terminal record as it is (state, result, unread mark, overflow mark, a reset or delete this server asked for) and drops the row of a missing one. A record still live, or one this server cannot read, is left as it is and the row stays that server's: the server still owns it, and a stopped server's unreadable record is dropped as before. A wait in progress is woken by the end and the run stream's watcher is told, as for a stopped server.
- **Only the background check pays for it.** The calls that read or add handles keep `endDeadForeign` (one liveness check per server, no record read). The cost is one record read per such handle every `peer_check_ms`, only while such handles are live; with `peer_check_ms` 0 an end a running server recorded is seen only once it stops.
- **Both servers can then hold the same unread signal.** That was already so for one loaded at `Open`; `deliverIOLocked` leaves out a signal whose record is already read on disk, and two deliveries at the same moment can still both return it.

Test: `TestAPeerCheckTakesTheEndsARunningPeerRecorded` (before the fix the wait on the peer's result did not return within 3 s); it also checks that a live and an unreadable record of the running peer stay running and untouched, a read result is not signalled, and a removed record drops the row.

## Native `code_context` tool (Go, 2026-11-07; decided by the user)

A native tool that keeps, per task, the exact source ranges a task needs, so the profiles working on it read them back instead of searching again. It follows the plan in `design_code_context.md`, narrowed by the user: it is a plain tool, with no `subagent` or `profile_message` hook, and no harness check at a profile boundary.

- **One command form.** An entry is `read(path, start, end)` (1-based, inclusive), a description, a priority 1–6, the SHA-256 of the whole file and of the range's exact bytes, `updated_by` (the sub-agent's name, else the session's profile) and the task generation of its last write. Actions: `read`, `update` (id kept), `drop`, `get`, `list`, `find`, `clear`. The tool never computes a range from edits; it only moves one to where identical bytes are (below).
- **Storage like `task` and `memory`.** One JSON file per task, `<storage_dir>/<task>.json`; the default `.code_context` is under the profile's `tool_storage` (the cwd without a profile); a configured relative `storage_dir` is from the cwd (changed 2026-11-08, below). An absolute `storage_dir` shares a task's entries across profiles and sessions; the same configured relative value now also lands in one cwd path, so it shares too. Writes are atomic and serialized per file within the process; separate processes are not coordinated (the same caveat as `task`).
- **Output.** Plain text, one line per entry: `#id p<priority> path:start-end [note]  description` (the note says stale and why, or where the code moved from; later description lines are indented). The hashes, writer and per-entry generation stay in the task file: the model has no use for them. `get` prints, per block, per block, every description, then the code between marker lines that carry a random token, so a marker-like line in the code cannot end a block.
- **Staleness is computed at each `get` and `list`, not stored.** Each distinct file is read once. An entry is current while its lines still hold the bytes it saved, even when another part of the file changed; otherwise it is stale (file gone, unreadable, too large, fewer lines, or other bytes) and is listed with its description and reason, never printed as code. A file reverted to the saved bytes is current again without a write. This is narrower than the plan's "file hash changed → stale": an edit elsewhere in the file no longer sends every entry of that file back to the caller.
- **Overlaps.** A `read` of an identical path and range returns the existing entry (with a description or priority, it changes that entry). `get` prints the current entries of one file whose ranges overlap or touch as one block (their union, each line once, every line taken from an entry that verified it) with all their descriptions.
- **Budget.** `max_output_bytes` (default 400000, about 100K tokens) bounds the code blocks of one `get`. Blocks go in priority order; once a block does not fit, every block of a higher priority number is left out too, and the left-out entries are listed by id for a `get` with `ids`.
- **Relocation by identical bytes (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** A stale entry whose saved bytes occur exactly once elsewhere in the file (as many lines, the same length, first-line hash and SHA-256) is moved there and the move is saved, so lines inserted above code no longer send it back to the caller. None or several occurrences leave it stale; a move onto a range another entry reads makes it stale with that entry's id. Each entry now also stores `content_bytes` and `head_sha256`; an entry written without them is never relocated. `relocate: false` turns it off.
- **`read` saves as it prints (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** `read` prints lines like a file read and saves them as an entry with an optional description and priority (default `default_priority`, 6). Lines an entry already holds unchanged are printed but not saved again; the exact range of a stale entry refreshes it. With a description the caller has already seen the code, so `read` saves the range without printing it; this replaced `add`.
- **`find` (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** `find` matches the query's words against the task's paths and descriptions, entries matching every word first, else those matching most, and checks each against its file. No separate index: the task file is the index, so it never disagrees with it.
- **Several operations per call (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** One `add` per range made a task cost dozens of calls, so `read` and `update` take `items` (at most `max_batch_items`, default 50); fields outside `items` are defaults for each item. The items are applied in order to the task in one store write: a wrong range in one item is reported on its line and does not stop the others, and the answer names what each item did. A call in which every item failed writes nothing (not even its `task_description`) and is a tool error, so the status line still says whether anything happened. A batch `read` stops printing code at `max_output_bytes` and keeps saving. `drop` and `get` keep `ids`.
- **Task list and task description (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** A call without arguments, or `list` without `task`, lists the tasks of the storage (most recent first, at most `max_scan_tasks`) with their descriptions, entry counts, generation and writer; no new action, since an empty call has nothing else to mean. A missing `action` means `list`, so `{"task":"T-1"}` is that task's list. The task description is a field of the task file (`description`, omitted when empty, so older files load unchanged), set with `task_description` on `read` or `update` and bounded by `max_description_chars`; `update` with only `task_description` sets it without an entry. The task list reads no code and so shows no staleness.
- **Slimmed (2026-11-07, decided by the user).** The tool had grown to the largest native schema (about 4.5 KB, 1.8 times `transcript`'s), so these were removed:
  - `since` deltas, the stats and the state file `<storage_dir>/.state/<task>.json` with `max_deliveries`. Every call and its result size are already in `messages.jsonl`, which is also the only place reads made with `exec` show; the state file cost a write on every `read`, `get` and `find`. A `.state` directory an earlier version left is ignored.
  - Reuse across tasks: `find` with `all_tasks`, `add` with `from_task`, and `read` borrowing another task's description, which scanned up to `max_scan_tasks` files on every `read`. `max_scan_tasks` now only bounds the task list.
  - `get` with `mode: outline` and `max_priority`: `list` shows every range and description without code.
  - `add`, folded into `read` with a description.
  - The schema's conditional rules (`allOf`/`if`/`then`): what each action requires is checked in Go (`missing_fields`, `invalid_field`), and the property descriptions were shortened. The schema is now about 1.9 KB.
  - YAML output with hashes, writer and generation per entry (above).
- **Ranges of ids (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** `ids` of `get` and `drop` also takes `"a-b"` (both included), alone or mixed with ids, so a large task is read in parts. A range skips ids no entry has; only a range naming no entry, or a missing single id, is reported. An id or range too large to parse refuses the call (`invalid_id`): it was first left out, so `get` with only such an id printed every entry and `drop` said it needed ids (found in review; `TestCodeContextIDRanges`). A range given twice is reported once. It replaces `get` with `path`, `start` and `end` (line ranges inside entries, added and reverted the same day as too complicated to use).
- **Stale ids in headers (added 2026-11-07, decided by the user).** The `get` and `list` headers name the stale ids (`3 stale (#1-2, #4)`), so the caller does not need a `list` or the end of a long `get` to find them. `read` and `update` check the task's other entries of the files they read, from the bytes already read (no extra file read, no other file, no relocation saved), and add `stale in the files read: #…` to the header, when the caller has just edited the code. `TestCodeContextStaleIDsInHeaders`. That made every `read` pay a full check, and a check scanned the file from its start once per entry (500 entries of a 4 MB file: about 1.8 s); entries now take their lines from the line offsets found once per file (about 9 ms; `BenchmarkCodeContextCheckManyEntries`, found in review).
- **A configured relative `storage_dir` is from the cwd (changed 2026-11-08, decided by the user: "storage_dir should be relative to cwd or absolute path. default to state profile dir").** It used to be joined onto the profile's `tool_storage`, so `"ctx"` landed in `<cwd>/.jad/state/<session>/<category>/<name>/tools/ctx`. Now an explicit relative value resolves from the session cwd, an absolute one is used as it is (it shares a store across profiles and sessions, and so does the same relative value now that it lands in one cwd path), and an omitted one keeps the default under the profile's `tool_storage` (the cwd without a profile). `~` is expanded and an empty string is still refused. The rule is one function, `normalizeToolStorageSubdir` (`sessionfs/tool_storage.go`), shared by `task`, `memory`, `subagent`, `code_context` and the `exec`/`bash`/`tool_smith` `spill.storage_dir`, so the five no longer drift. Migration: a config with an explicit relative `storage_dir` now opens a different store; entries written to the old place under `tool_storage` are not moved or read, so move them by hand or point `storage_dir` at the old absolute path. Not changed: `tool_storage` and `tool_storage_dir` (already cwd-relative) and the deprecated `stdout_full`/`stderr_full` spill templates, whose relative targets are still resolved under `tool_storage`, so a relative template and a relative `storage_dir` now differ.
- **Not built from the plan.** `read_at_snapshot` (audit only), a stored `stale` flag and `stale_since_generation` (staleness is computed), and the harness check before the next profile's first request.

Config: `tools.native.code_context` with `storage_dir`, `paths` (read_style), `max_entries`, `max_description_chars`, `max_range_lines`, `max_file_bytes`, `max_output_bytes`, `max_storage_bytes`, `relocate`, `default_priority`, `max_find_results`, `max_scan_tasks`, `max_batch_items`. Tests: `internal/tools/native/code_context_test.go`, `code_context_features_test.go`, `code_context_batch_test.go`, `code_context_tasks_test.go` and `code_context_ids_test.go` (through the catalog and `sample/tools/native/code_context.json`) and `internal/sessionfs/native_code_context_settings_test.go`.

## The native `memory` tool is now `records` (Go, 2026-11-08; decided by the user)

- **Why.** The tool stores bounded JSON-object records; "memory" was easy to confuse with process memory, the agent's own memory and the memory MCP server. Hard rename, no alias, as `decide` became `decide_systemone`.
- **What moved.** Tool id `native.records`, name `records`, settings key `tools.native.records`, default store directory `.records`, definition file `sample/tools/native/records.json`, the statecontext provider key `records` (in `statecontext.triggers.*.tools` and in the snapshot JSON), the store kind of the resolver cache, the `HarnessPaths` field, the schemas and the profile `tools` patterns. Go: `memory*.go` became `records*.go`, `MemoryStore`/`MemoryItem`/`MemoryStoreError`/`NewMemoryTool`/`CollectMemoryStateContext` became `Records*`, `NativeMemorySettings` and `normalizeNativeMemory*` became `NativeRecords*`/`normalizeNativeRecords*`, `SessionConfig.NativeMemory` became `NativeRecords`. Error codes are `records_storage_error`, `records_conflict` and `records_operation_failed`.
- **User-visible text.** The noun changed: "Record created/retrieved/updated/deleted", "N record(s) listed/found", "Records require a valid operation and type", "Invalid arguments for records", "record was not found", "record cursor is invalid" and so on. The four frozen goldens `messages-native-records-{create,get,list,invalid-create}.jsonl` and `native-records-definition.json` were regenerated in the same commit (call ids `call-records-*`, ref placeholder `RECORDS_REF`); the content is otherwise byte for byte what it was. This is the rename's own contract change, not a weakened test.
- **Old names fail loudly.** `tools.native.memory` is in `removedNativeTools`, so it is refused with `tools.native.memory was removed; memory was renamed to records; move the settings to tools.native.records and use the name records in tool lists; delete the key`; a `memory` entry in a profile's `tools.allow/deny/lazy`, a sub-agent's `tools` or a `tool_smith` script list is refused with the same advice ("remove it from the list"). A statecontext provider `memory` is refused as an unknown provider. A model call by the old id `memory` is `unknown_tool`; no deprecated alias exists.
- **No data migration.** An existing `.memory` store keeps its files and is not read. The new `.records` default starts empty. To keep the old records, stop the backend and copy once: `cp -a <tool_storage>/.memory/. <tool_storage>/.records/` (or point `tools.native.records.storage_dir` at the old directory). Sessions already compacted carry a snapshot with the key `memory`; the next snapshot differs (the provider key is part of the hashed bytes) and is written afresh.
- **Deliberate exception.** The store's internal sidecar names `.memory-sequence` (the ref counter) and `.memory-cursors` (persisted cursors) are unchanged, so a copied store keeps its ref numbering and cursors instead of restarting at 1. The process-local lock key (`records:` + root) and temp names are not persisted and did change.
- **Left alone on purpose.** `internal/memorytrace`, `run_content_memory_bytes`/`RunContentMemoryBytes`, `memory_retention`/session/bash-output/transcript memory benchmarks, `schemaLocation = "memory://native-tool/schema.json"`, `sample/jad/mcp/code/codebase-memory-mcp.json`, and wording about heap or in-memory data. Older sections of this file keep describing the tool as `memory`, as it was then.
- **Restart.** The live harness files (`.jad/tools/jad/*.json`, `.jad/profiles/test/default.json`, two skill prompts; all gitignored) were changed to the new names; the running backend still has the old definition loaded until it is restarted.

## Native `task` redesign: batch `ops`, list paging and search, cross-process lock (Go, 2026-11-08; decided by the user)

The user approved Option B of `task-redesign.md` with Q1 yes, Q2 no, Q3 all or nothing, Q4 yes, Q5 yes, Q6 stateless `offset`. The tool keeps its name and its flat single-call shape; Option C (owner-scoped store, log, sidecars), `$n` back-references and `ReadOnly` on the task tool were rejected. `tasks.json`, `schema_version` 1, unknown-field preservation and id allocation are unchanged.

- **Surface.** New fields `q` (every word is a case-insensitive substring of the title, a tag or a document path; the `recordsContainsTerms` rule), `offset`, `detail` (document paths and ` | updated: <ts>` in list lines) and `ops`. The three top-level `allOf` rules are gone (Q1): `Execute` already enforced them with its own codes (`missing_title`, `missing_task_id`, `missing_update`), and Anthropic stripped them anyway. The schema is 1,243 bytes compact (1,747 before). **Node golden parity ends here:** `messages-native-task-invalid-add.jsonl` and `...-invalid-update.jsonl` (they pinned `#/allOf/...` ajv paths) and `testdata/native-task-definition.json` were rewritten by hand; the add golden is still the Node bytes. A flat `{"action":"add"}` is now an execution error `missing_title`, not a schema error.
- **Batch.** `ops` holds 1 to `max_batch_ops` task objects (default 20, hard max 200), each normalized like a flat call and validated by the same code (`parseTaskCall`), run in order against one loaded document and written once. All or nothing (Q3): the first op that breaks a rule, names a missing id or hits a store limit stops the batch before the write; the result is `batch_failed` with `failed_op` (1-based), `op_error_code`, `op_error`, `open_ids` and `applied: 0`, in the failing op's status. `ops` with any other top-level field is `invalid_batch`; too many is `batch_too_large` (both carry `applied: 0` and no `failed_op`). Mutation lines omit title and tags; `get` prints the list-style line; a `list` prints `N list K of T[, next_offset M]` and its lines indented by two spaces. List sections share `max_list_bytes`; mutation lines are never cut. All ops are validated before the first one runs, so a rule error in op 5 is reported even if op 2 would have named a missing id.
- **Summary.** `N ops applied` when the document was written, `N ops run` when no op changed anything (reads, `done` on done tasks).
- **List.** Information lines `action`, `count`, `total`, and, only when matches remain (cut by `limit` or by `max_list_bytes`), `has_more` and `next_offset` (`offset + count`). An `offset` past the total lists 0 tasks and shows `total`. The store filters and sorts first and clones only the returned page (the 3000-task benchmark cloned every match before).
- **Store.** `TaskStore.Apply(ops, policy)` is the engine: lock, one load, in-memory ops, one write. `Create`, `Update`, `Delete`, `Get` and `List` are wrappers. A miss writes nothing (it used to rewrite the file and could fail on the byte cap), and `done` on a done task writes nothing and keeps `updated_at` (it used to reorder it). `update` always bumps `updated_at`.
- **Lock.** Every load, and every load-modify-write, holds the in-process mutex and then `sessionlock.Acquire` on `<dir>/.tasks.json.jad-lock` (Q4), so two backends sharing a storage directory cannot lose updates. The lock file is persistent (the sessionlock rule) and lives in the storage directory the checkpoints already exclude. A read of a storage directory that does not exist yet takes no lock file, so reading never creates the directory (statecontext reads on every run). `write` now `Sync`s the temporary file before the rename, so a power cut leaves the old file or the new one. The lock wait uses a background context: it ends when the other holder releases or dies (flock), not when the run is cancelled.
- **Ids.** `1`, `T-1`, `t1` and `T-001` all mean `T-001`; the form is canonical (`%03d`), so normalizing twice changes nothing. Anything that is not a positive integer after that stays `invalid_task_id`.
- **`task_not_found`.** `available_ids` is now `open_ids`: ids of tasks that are not done, in list order, at most `list_limit`, then ` (+N more)`; none when nothing is open. They come from the read that missed, not a second `List`; in a batch they come from the stored file, so they never name a task an earlier op of the failed batch would have created. (The older entry above that lists `available_ids` for task describes the previous behavior.)
- **Manifest (Q5).** A definition file with the previous schema (`taskInputSchemaV1`, kept in `task_schema_v1.go`) still loads (`requireTaskDefinition`, the `requireExecDefinition` pattern); the model is shown the current schema, and the current description when the file still has the old one. Any other schema keeps the "replace with sample/tools/native/task.json" error. The V1 path is permanent code.
- **Setting.** `tools.native.task.max_batch_ops` (1 to 200, default 20), in the loader, `sample/schemes/setting.schema.json` and the settings tests. No key was removed or renamed.
- **Unchanged.** Statecontext (`TaskStateSource.List` with `Statuses` and `Limit`; the items-mode JSON is pinned by a test), checkpoint exclusion, shared store per directory, no owner stamp (Q2), default `ordered` tool scheduling (which already serialized same-response task calls; the proposal's assumption that it was not the default was wrong).
- **Tests.** `tools/native/task_batch_test.go`, `task_list_query_test.go`, `catalog_task_test.go`, `task_golden_test.go`, `valid_values_errors_test.go`; `sessionfs/native_task_settings_test.go`, `native_tool_schema_test.go`.

### Native `task` redesign, review fixes (Go, 2026-11-08)

A review of the redesign commit changed these points; everything else in the record above stands.

- **List paging cannot stall.** The first list line of a page is shown even when it alone is larger than `max_list_bytes` (before, a budget smaller than one line gave `count: 0`, `has_more` and `next_offset == offset`, so a client that follows `next_offset` looped forever). In a batch the first list line of the call is shown; a later section takes what is left of the shared budget and can show none; its header then ends with `, over budget, ask again alone` and has no `next_offset` (it would equal the section's own offset, and a client that reissued the batch would loop). Asked alone, that section is the first list of its call and shows a line. No code or test outside `tools/native` parses the batch header, so no field was kept for compatibility. `max_list_bytes` is therefore a budget for pages of more than one line, not a hard cap on one line.
- **A read takes no lock file while `tasks.json` does not exist** (it was: while the directory did not exist). A read of an existing but empty storage directory left `.tasks.json.jad-lock` behind. A writer publishes `tasks.json` by an atomic rename, so a read that sees no file is a valid answer without the lock. Reads of an existing file still lock, as decided above.
- **`Apply` contract in the wrappers.** `oneTaskResult` is the one reader of a single-op outcome: a miss is `found == false`, and an outcome that is neither a miss nor exactly one result is a `TaskStoreError` (`task_storage_error` in the tool) instead of an index out of range. `Create` and `List` used to return a zero value with a nil error in that case. The concrete store cannot produce such an outcome; this keeps the wrappers and `executeNativeTask` from depending on it.
- **Kept as decided.** The lock wait still uses a background context (an earlier entry above). `"ops": null` is a schema error (`validation_failed`, `/ops` must be array) like a null `title` or `id`; `invalid_batch` is for `ops` next to another field, and for non-array, empty or non-object `ops` when `Execute` is called without the schema (tests, direct callers). `max_batch_ops` 1 to 200 (default 20), `list_limit`/`max_list_limit` 1 to 10000, `max_list_bytes` and `max_storage_bytes` 1 to 10 MiB, `max_tasks` 1 to 100000 agree between the loader and `sample/schemes/setting.schema.json`.
- **Tests.** `task_store_contract_test.go`: two real child processes (the sessionlock re-exec pattern) against the lock, four processes adding concurrently, the `Apply` outcome contract, `oneTaskResult`, paging that never stalls, null `ops`, a read of an empty directory.

## `load_tool` is a runner: lazy tools never enter the tools array (Go, 2026-11-08; decided by the user; T-102)

The user accepted the design and D1 to D7 of T-102 (`missing.md`: a load broke the provider's prompt-cache prefix, because a loaded tool was added to the tools array of the next request, and the array comes before the system prompt and the history).

- **Now.** `load_tool` is always listed, `ReadOnly`, never lazy. Flat schema (no `oneOf`: the Anthropic adapter strips it), `additionalProperties:false`: `names` (describe: name, id, description, `inputSchema` of the matched lazy tools, patterns allowed, bounded by `tools.lazy_load_max` and the per-tool schema bounds MCP already enforces), `run` (execute one lazy tool by its exact model-facing name or `mcp:<server>:<tool>`; a pattern is refused), `arguments` (for `run`, default `{}`). No `names` and no `run` lists `name — summary` lines. `names` with `run`, or `arguments` without `run`, is `invalid_input`. A lazy tool's `Listed` is a constant false, so no tool's use changes the tools array: the bytes before the history are the same in every round.
- **Run.** `load_tool run` dispatches the target through the catalog that executes it (`ExecutionContext.Dispatcher`, set by `Catalog.ExecuteFunctionCall`; `Has` filters), with the outer `call_id`, the execution context unchanged (not `ForNested`) and `ViaRunner` set, and returns the target's own output value (a content array with images stays one). A plan-mode or `Only`-narrowed catalog answers `unknown_tool` for what it dropped, and `list` and `describe` leave those out. The target's schema check runs as for an eager tool; its `invalid_input` stays model-visible. `WithLazyGate` now refuses every call that is not `ViaRunner` (`tool_not_loaded`, kept as the code; the text points at `load_tool run <name>`).
- **Judgments.** New `Tool.Delegate` (a pure parse of the runner's arguments) and `Catalog.ResolveCall`; `ChangesState`, `IsLeafCall`, `CallMayChangeFiles`, `IsPlanGuardedCall`, `SourceOf`, `IsUserDecisionCall` and `roundChangesWorkspace` judge a `load_tool run` as its target: `run exec` takes the checkpoint by the name `exec`, `run mcp:...` by source under `include_mcp_tools`, a leaf target takes a leaf token for the whole call and runs inline (`load_tool` itself holds no `LeafBudget`), and `ImageResizer.Apply` sees source `mcp` for an image a run returns. A catalog with no `Delegate` skips the parse.
- **Hooks (D2).** A `run` call fires the target's pre/post hooks once, at the nested dispatch, and not `load_tool`'s; `list`, `describe` and an invalid combination fire `load_tool`'s. `load_tool` returns its text as an output value (it must, to pass a target's content array through), so the hook wrapper now appends its report to a text output and tells the post hook the text, not its JSON encoding (`appendOutputText`, `outputResultText`). A direct call to a lazy name is refused inside the tool's wrappers, so its hooks fire once, as before. A `run` the runner refuses before dispatch (a pattern, an unknown name) fires no hook.
- **tool_smith (D6).** `load_tool` joins `NativeToolSmithExcludedTools`. Consequence: a script's nested call to a lazy tool is never `ViaRunner`, so it is refused whatever the set holds. Before, a script could call a lazy tool once the model had loaded it.
- **Config and state (D4).** No new key. `tools.lazy`, `lazy_summary_bytes`, `lazy_load_max` (now bounds a describe batch; schema text updated) and `tools.native.load_tool: {}` work as before. `lazy_tools.json` is still opened and rewritten when the configuration changes, but nothing writes a loaded tool and nothing reads the set to list or gate. The write path (`LazySet.Load*`) is dead code kept for a later cleanup.
- **Manifest (D1).** `sample/tools/native/load_tool.json` changed with the Go schema. A copied older file fails `requireDefinition` at startup with the existing "replace with sample/tools/native/load_tool.json" message; accepted.
- **Tests.** `tools/delegate_test.go`, `tools/lazy_test.go`, `session/runner_load_tool_test.go`, `native/lazy_load_tool_run_test.go`, `native/lazy_load_tool_test.go`, and the app tests that pinned load-and-list (`lazy_tools_integration_test.go`, `native_load_tool_test.go`, `child_lazy_load_test.go`, `profile_message_lazy_test.go`, `remote_subagent_lazy_test.go`). The app fixture's `providerRequest.RawTools` keeps each tools element byte for byte; `TestToolsArrayIsByteIdenticalAcrossListDescribeAndRun` compares every request of a run.

### Open (T-102)

Closed by T-103 (2026-11-08); each line points at its entry below. One item stays, reworded as a non-goal.

- ~~**Per-server MCP order.**~~ Closed: `toolsFromList` sorts each server's tools by name (T-103, "MCP order").
- ~~**Tools route (D5).**~~ Closed: the route marks lazy tools with `lazy: true` (T-103, "Tools route").
- ~~**`lazy_tools.json` cleanup (D4).**~~ Closed: the file, the fingerprint and `LazySet.Load*` are gone (T-103, "No state file").
- ~~**`profile_message` and remote subagent** are verified by tests that read the state folder~~ Kept as a non-goal, see "Not closed" in T-103: the tests no longer read a state file (there is none); the second-server path is still not run live.
- **Accuracy unmeasured.** Kept as a non-goal (T-103, "Not closed"): it needs live models.
- ~~**`tool_smith` scripts** cannot reach a lazy tool any more~~ Closed: the bridge's dispatch is a runner's (T-103, "tool_smith scripts").
- ~~**`tool_not_loaded`** keeps its code~~ Closed: renamed `lazy_tool_not_loaded` (T-103, "Refusal code").
- ~~**Race run of `tools/native`.**~~ Closed: the whole package ran under `-race` once (T-103, "Race run").

## `tools.lazy` is MCP and extension only; the T-102 gaps are closed; the fork memory test measures retained heap (Go, 2026-11-08; decided by the user; T-103)

### Fork memory test measures retained heap

- **Why it flaked.** `allocatedBy` sampled process-wide `HeapAlloc` every 100 us at GOGC 5 and subtracted the heap after one collection. `HeapAlloc` includes garbage not yet collected, so the number followed how fast the collector got CPU: idle the message fork read 4.3-4.8 MB of an 8.4 MB bound, with 12 CPU burners 5.9-6.6 MB, and it failed at 9.1 MB in the full suite.
- **Rule now.** `retainedBy` (in `session_fork_memory_test.go`) takes a sample as the lowest `HeapAlloc` of three forced collections in a row, at GOGC 1 for the duration, and reports the highest sample above the starting one. What is left after a collection is what is reachable plus the garbage the running fork allocated during that mark (floating garbage); the lowest of three drops it, and GOGC 1 keeps the mutator from out-allocating a collection (a first version without the GOGC change read 14-20 MB for a clean fork under load; with GOGC 1 and one reading per sample, 2.4-8.6 MB). The test no longer holds the 16.8 MB source transcript while it measures (it reads it after), so the heap being collected is small. A fork that buffers the transcript keeps it reachable until it returns, so every sample sees it; a transient line buffer a streaming fork drops at once may fall between samples, which is the point: nothing is retained.
- **Numbers (bytes, whole fork / message fork, bound 8,395,634).** Idle, 3 runs: 1.09-1.09 MB / 2.17-2.70 MB. Under 12 CPU burners, 10 runs in a row: 1.44-3.59 MB / 2.21-4.83 MB. `-race -count=3`: 1.08 MB / 2.16 MB. Mutations: the whole transcript copied into a slice in `writeFork`'s line visitor: 16.57 MB (whole) and 16.01 MB (by message); in `messageTurnCursor`'s read loop: 1.09 MB / 18.14 MB. Both CAUGHT by `mutate-check.sh`, about 2x over the bound against at most 0.6x clean under load.
- **Code is right.** The message fork holds about one turn (raw records, their normalized messages, the decoded line: roughly 4.5 MB for a 512 KiB turn); it does not hold more than half the transcript, so only the test changed.

### `tools.lazy` takes MCP and extension tools only

- **Rule.** `tools.CanBeLazy(definition)` is true for `Source` `mcp` or `extension`. `LazyEntryMatches` (EntryMatches and CanBeLazy) is what `LazySet.isLazy`, `Bind`, `ProfileShows`/`lazyAllows` and the app's entry check use; `GateFor` also refuses a native definition. `EntryMatches` itself is unchanged, so `tools.allow` and `tools.deny` still name native tools. A native tool (`skill`, `subagent`, `task`, `exec`, `read_image`, `records`, `load_tool` and the rest, the late-built `transcript`, `transcripts`, `profile`, `profile_message` included) is always listed, called by its name, and `load_tool run <native tool>` answers `unknown_lazy_tool` ("is not a lazy tool of this profile").
- **Breaking config change.** A profile whose `tools.lazy` names a native tool no longer loads: `paths.profiles.<name>.tools.lazy[<i>] "exec" is a native tool and cannot be lazy: tools.lazy takes MCP tools (mcp:<server>:<tool> or mcp:<server>:*) and extension tools (decide_systemone, web_search)`; `load_tool` keeps its own message (`... cannot be lazy: it is the tool that loads the others`). Fix: remove the native entries from `tools.lazy` (they stay in the tools array). `sample/schemes/profile.schema.json`'s lazy pattern is now `^(web_search|decide_systemone|mcp:...)$`, so an editor flags it too; a test pins schema and loader against every native name. The check on registered definitions (`checkLazyEntriesVisible`) also refuses a native entry, in case a definition reaches it some other way.
- **Correction of the brief.** `*` and `mcp:*` were never valid `tools.lazy` entries (the loader refused them as "not a native tool", and `EntryMatches` never matched them); `*` is a `load_tool names` pattern only. Nothing was added: `load_tool names ["*"]` describes every bound lazy tool, which now means MCP and extension tools only (tests: `TestMatchStarSelectsOnlyBoundMCPAndExtensionTools`, `TestNativeToolStaysEagerBesideLazyExtensionAndMCPTools`).
- **Removed.** `lazyLate` (late native tools are plain hooked tools), `NotLazyBindable`, the nested-subagent re-gate (`regateNestedSelf`; `nestedSelf` only hooks), and the native part of the lazy pass. The lazy pass in `buildNativeTools` stays for extension tools; MCP tools keep `GateLazyTools` and the children's `GateFor`.
- **Text.** `load_tool`'s description (Go and `sample/tools/native/load_tool.json`), the `names` schema text, `setting.schema.json` (tools.native.load_tool), `agent.schema.json` and the profile schema's `lazy` text say it covers MCP and extension tools only.
- **Tests.** Config refusal for every native name and acceptance of `web_search`, `decide_systemone`, `mcp:<server>:<tool>` and `mcp:<server>:*` (`TestProfileToolsRefusesANativeToolAsLazy`); `LazyEntryMatches`/`CanBeLazy`; a set handed native entries binds, gates and matches none (`TestANativeToolCanNeverBeLazy`, `TestGateForRefusesANativeToolWithABoundID`); late native tools, a child of a parent whose set is handed `profile_message`/`read_image`, and nested children keep native tools eager (`catalog_late_lazy_test.go`, `catalog_tool_hooks_test.go`, `subagent_depth_test.go`); an extension tool lazily listed, described and run through `load_tool`, with the tools array byte-identical (`TestLazyExtensionToolIsListedDescribedAndRunThroughLoadTool`); a native `skill` listed and called directly beside lazy tools; the `session` runner tests use MCP and extension targets. The `app` fixture gained `__web_search` (an extension `web_search` against a local searxng-style server). `catalog_filter_test.go` and `catalog_load_tool_test.go` needed no change.

### No state file

- `lazy_tools.json`, `lazyToolsState`, `LazyFingerprint`, `LazySet.Load`/`LoadAll`/`LoadMatches`/`Loaded` and the mutex are removed; `OpenLazySet(stateDir, ...)` became `NewLazySet(entries, summaryBytes, shown)`, which cannot fail and writes nothing. No file is created for a new session; an old `lazy_tools.json` is left where it is and never read. `Bind`, `Gate`, `GateFor`, `Entries`, `Match` and `SetLazy` stay. `tools.lazy` no longer needs a catalog layout (the state folder was its only reason), so a session without one opens a set like any other.
- Tests that read the file now assert it does not exist (`requireNoLazyFile`, in the app, `profile_message` and remote subagent tests); `TestLazyLoadToolKeepsTheStateFileUnchanged` and the fingerprint, corrupt-file and reload tests went with the file.

### MCP order

- `toolsFromList` sorts each server's tools by name (stable, on a copy) before the deny list, duplicate and size checks; resource and prompt tools keep their fixed order after them. A server that lists in another order no longer changes the tools array. `TestToolsFromListSortsByNameWhateverTheServerOrder` encodes the array from a listing and its reverse and compares the bytes; with the sort removed it fails. Across servers the order is still the configured one.

### Tools route

- `GET /api/sessions/<ref>/tools` rows are `tools.RouteDefinition` (a `Definition` and `lazy`, omitted when false); `Catalog.RouteDefinitions()` lists the listed tools and the lazy ones in registry order, and `Resolver.ListTools` answers with it. `Tool.Lazy` (set by `WithLazyGate`) is the marker; it is not a field of `Definition`, and `ResponseTools` builds its own struct, so the provider payload is unchanged (`TestRouteDefinitionsMarkLazyToolsAndLeaveTheProviderArrayAlone` compares the array byte for byte against a catalog that never had the lazy tool; `TestToolsArrayIsByteIdenticalAcrossListDescribeAndRun` still holds). `hidden` is unchanged. The frontend type `ToolDefinition` gained an optional `lazy`; nothing reads it yet.

### tool_smith scripts

- `toolSmithBridge.handleFrame` dispatches with `ViaRunner` set, on a copy of the bridge's execution context and only there (`ForNested` still clears it). The gate is the allowlist (`byName`: `tools.native.tool_smith.tools`, minus `NativeToolSmithExcludedTools`, minus what the profile hides); `load_tool` stays excluded. So a script's call of a lazy extension tool it names runs; a lazy tool it does not name is refused as not allowed; the model's direct call is still refused (`TestToolSmithScriptRunsAnAllowlistedLazyExtensionToolOnly`; the mutation that drops `ViaRunner` fails it). Only extension tools can be reached: `tools.native.tool_smith.tools` still takes native and extension names, not MCP ids, as before T-102, so a lazy MCP tool is not reachable from a script and nothing was widened.

### Refusal code

- `tool_not_loaded` is now `lazy_tool_not_loaded` (producer in `tools/lazy.go`, the profile schema sentence, the design note, and every Go test that matched it). Nothing in the frontend or docs outside the repo notes consumed it. The older entries of this file keep the old name as history.

### Race run

- `go test -race -count=1 -timeout 25m ./internal/tools/native` ran the whole package once: exit 0, `ok ... 226.805s` (3:56 wall clock), on the final code of this change, so the old `-run` filter is no longer needed. The final verification also ran the other touched packages under `-race`.

### Not closed

- **Accuracy unmeasured (non-goal).** Whether models use list, describe, run as well as the old load-then-call flow cannot be measured without live models. The description and the refusal text are the only guidance. This is a decision not to measure it here, not a task left half done.
- **`profile_message` and a remote subagent through a live second server** are still verified through the app fixtures only (a target profile and a remote subagent with their own lazy MCP tools, run through scripted providers). A live second server needs a deployment this suite does not have.
- ~~**A `load_tool run <exec>` is still judged as exec by the checkpoint.**~~ Closed by T-104 ("ResolveCall resolves only a lazy-capable target"). `Catalog.ResolveCall` resolves the target by name in the catalog, so the checkpoint is taken for a `run` that the runner then refuses (a native tool is not lazy). It is the conservative direction (one extra snapshot, no lost one) and was not changed. Closing it would make `ResolveCall` skip a target that is not a lazy tool.

## Review of the lazy-tool commits: fixes and the items left open (Go, 2026-11-08; T-104)

Review of b167c9c4, 0e7ff0f1, 4d41fe8a and 9b29855f against their production paths and callers. The T-102/T-103 decisions (D1 to D7, the hook rule, the reachability rule, no new key, the breaking `tools.lazy` rule, no state file, `load_tool` out of tool_smith) are unchanged.

### Found and fixed

- **Fork memory test leaked its sampler (medium, test only).** `retainedBy` closed the sampler's `done` channel only after `call()` returned, so a `t.Fatalf` inside the measured fork (`runtime.Goexit`) left a goroutine forcing three collections in a loop for the rest of the `app` package run. The sampling is now `sampleRetained(call, sample)`, which stops and waits for the sampler in a `defer` (a `sync.Once` shared with the normal path); `retainedBy` passes `liveHeap`, the same lowest-of-three sample as before. `TestSampleRetainedStopsTheSamplerWhenTheCallExits` exits the measured call with `runtime.Goexit` and fails when a sample is taken afterwards (removing the `defer stop()`: CAUGHT). The measurement is unchanged and still catches retention: clean 1.09 MB (whole) / 2.17 MB (by message) against the 8,395,634 bound; the whole transcript held by `writeFork`'s visitor 17.6 MB / 17.1 MB, the turn buffer of `messageTurnCursor` never released 1.10 MB / 34.2 MB, both CAUGHT.
- **ResolveCall resolves only a lazy-capable target (low; closes the last T-103 item).** `ResolveCall` judged `load_tool run <native tool>` (by name or by ID, e.g. `exec`, `native.exec`, `request_draft`) as that native tool, although the runner only runs a bound lazy entry and answers `unknown_lazy_tool`: an extra checkpoint snapshot, ordered scheduling, a leaf token, and (for `request_draft`) every other call of the response replaced by a `user_decision_required` placeholder, for a call that runs nothing. It now resolves only a target with `CanBeLazy` (MCP or extension). Safe, no lost checkpoint: the runner executes a target only when `LazySet.Match` finds exactly one bound entry, a bound entry passed `isLazy`, so `LazyEntryMatches`, so `CanBeLazy`; it dispatches that entry's model-facing name through the same catalog the judgments read (`Dispatcher` is the executing catalog), and a model-facing name is one tool whose source is fixed (MCP names are `mcp_<server>_<tool>_<hash>`, extension names are fixed and no native name is either), so a call the runner executes is always resolved; a call it is not resolved for runs nothing. Tests: `TestResolveCallLeavesARunOfANativeToolAlone` and two new cases of `TestRoundChangesWorkspaceJudgesALoadToolRunByItsTarget` (`run exec`, `run native.exec`); dropping the `CanBeLazy` check fails both (CAUGHT). The `delegate_test.go` targets became extension tools, which is what a run can target.
- **Subagent fingerprint ignored whether a tool is lazy (low).** `subagentFingerprint` hashed `Catalog.Definitions()`, which does not carry the lazy marker, while `configChangedNote` says the compared scope includes the lazy list. A tool that became lazy (it leaves the child's tools array and runs only through `load_tool`) gave no note on resume. It now hashes `RouteDefinitions()`: the same rows with `lazy` set for a lazy tool and omitted otherwise, so a catalog without lazy tools hashes exactly as before and older manifests still compare. `TestFingerprintChangesWhenAToolBecomesLazy` pins both (reverting to `Definitions()`: fails).
- **Dead code.** `Catalog.ListedDefinitions` had no production caller after 9b29855f (the route uses `RouteDefinitions`); removed, and its three test uses read the non-lazy route rows.
- **Text drift.** `profile.schema.json` (`tools.lazy` no longer needs a catalog layout), `NativeLoadToolSettings` ("lists, describes and runs"), the `bindMCP` comment (no child loads a lazy tool any more), the resolver comment on the child set (no state folder), the `roundChangesWorkspace` comment (a run of `exec` is not judged as exec), the misplaced doc comments of `outputResultText`/`appendOutputText`, and the T-103 date in the banner of `audit_findings/design_lazy_tools.md`. `audit_findings/lazy_tools_review.md` is a review of older commits and stays as history.
- **Frontend type.** `ToolDefinition.source` in `frontend_new/src/api.ts` now includes `extension`, which the route sends. `lazy?` stays declared: it is what the route sends, and `fetchSessionTools` has no caller in `frontend_new` yet, so nothing displays either field.
- **Old state file.** `TestAnOldLazyStateFileIsIgnoredAndLeftAlone` writes an older `lazy_tools.json` that names the lazy MCP tool as loaded: the session runs, the direct call is still refused with `lazy_tool_not_loaded`, the run works, the tools array is the same in every request and the file is byte for byte unchanged.

### Checked, no change

- **ViaRunner.** Written only by `runLazyTool` and the tool_smith bridge, each on its own copy of the execution, read only by `WithLazyGate`. Every path that starts another run or a nested catalog clears it: the subagent child (`ForNested` in `subagent_call.go`, which the child run's `ToolExecution` is), the bridge's base execution (`ForNested`), `profile_message` (its request is built from fields, not the execution), the remote API (a fresh execution), the HTTP run executor (a fresh execution). The only `ExecuteFunctionCall` callers are the session round, the remote API, the bridge and `DispatchCall` (runner and the gate's inner single-tool catalog). The model sees lazy tools only through `load_tool` list/describe; `ResponseTools` skips them (`Listed` false is set only by `WithLazyGate`), and `Definitions()` (hook-match warnings, the profile entry checks, the fingerprint) never reaches the model.
- **Judgments.** By name and by ID agree with the runner (`Match` by name, or by ID for `mcp:`; `byTarget` by name first, then ID; no MCP or extension name contains `:`). A runner target, an unknown target, a pattern and a target the executing catalog lacks (plan mode, `Only`) are left unchanged and judged as `load_tool`, which is right: the runner refuses them (`unknown_lazy_tool`, `invalid_input`, `unknown_tool`).
- **Plan mode and Only.** A lazy MCP tool is not `ReadOnly`, so plan mode drops it (unless an allow entry keeps it whole) and `load_tool` neither lists nor runs it. `web_search` and `decide_systemone` are `ReadOnly` and stay, lazy or eager: the same rule as for an eager tool.
- **Hooks.** A run fires the target's hooks once (the parent's copy is `hooked(gated(real))`, the gate's inner catalog holds the unhooked tool; children hook after `GateFor`; the bridge holds the same hooked copies). A hook rewrite cannot reach past the runner: `runLazyTool` re-parses and re-checks whatever arguments it runs with.
- **Lazy scope.** `CanBeLazy` gates `LazyEntryMatches`, `isLazy`/`Bind`, `lazyAllows`, `GateFor` and the app check; the loader (`checkLazyEntry`) and the schema pattern agree (pinned by the T-103 tests).
- **MCP order.** The sort runs on a clone before the checks; deny, duplicate (by name, a set), nil entry, ids and the feature tools after the server tools are as before. Only which error is reported first, when a listing has several, now follows name order.
- **Error surfaces.** `lazy_tool_not_loaded` (gate, inside the hooks), `invalid_input` and `unknown_lazy_tool` (runner), `unknown_tool` (runner for a dropped tool, catalog for an unknown name), `validation_failed` (the target's schema, at the outer dispatch, named for the target) and `batch_too_large` (describe) each come from the layer that knows the reason.

### Open

- **A toolset child's MCP tool is swapped for the owner's session tool when the IDs match (pre-existing since b296be92, not fixed).** `bindMCP` calls `s.lazy.GateFor(tool)` for toolset tools too, and `GateFor` returns the gate of the set's bound entry, the owner's session tool, not of the tool it is given. A child whose toolset has a server with the same ID as an owner's lazy server therefore runs the session's connection (its config, cwd and sandbox wrapper) instead of the toolset's, without the toolset's hooks. Confirmed with a throwaway probe (the child's call answered from the session tool); not caused by the four commits and not an escape (the swapped tool is still gated).
